| 37 | Ship `components/ui/Button.tsx` and migrate the 10 funnel screens | **M** |
| 38 | Raise `--text-xs` floor; bump header/auth/filter/portal tap targets to 44px | **S** |

### WAVE 3 — conversion (≈ 8–12 dev-days)
- Wellness consumer catalogue + enrol + `GET /wellness/enrollments/mine` + "My classes" ⚠ **(L)**
- URL state for `/temples` and `/astrology` filters → shareable, bookmarkable, back-button-correct, **indexable facet pages** (the SEO payoff) **(M)**
- Sitewide heading ramp: one `h1` per page, three archetype recipes, fix the level skips, add the missing `h1`s **(M)**
- Cool-gray → warm-surface codemod (122 files) + radius/container consolidation + lint rules banning off-token utilities **(M)**
- Remove the silent astrology fallback and give every portal dashboard a real error state **(S)**
- Admin: subscriptions from the real table, IST day boundaries, reports status filters, provider names on `/admin/revenue`, remove or implement the 5 stub endpoints **(M)**
- Blog + gurukul seed content; `/wellness/instructors` index; fix the dead `/refunds` instructions **(S)**
- Mobile rebuild: envelope accessors, delete every mock fallback, real interfaces, zod schemas on every hook, gate `/subscribe` behind payment, in-app cart and booking **(L)**
- Security headers into `next.config.ts` (not `public/_headers`), `poweredByHeader: false`, retire the localStorage token in favour of the existing httpOnly cookie **(M)**
- The 48h video-proof auto-refund sweep, or remove the guarantee **(M)**

## 10. NEEDS THE FOUNDER

Only-a-human items. Exact dashboard and value.

| # | Action | Where | Value / decision |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | **Rotate the exposed Supabase service-role key** | Supabase → project `hlkvfarvfblhzhhnhtbp` → Settings → API → `service_role` → Reset | Then set it **only** in the Render dashboard, never in a repo file. Two agents connected to prod from a local checkout before noticing. |
| 2 | **Rotate the production JWT secret, Groq key and Cohere key** | Groq console → API Keys; Cohere dashboard → API Keys; JWT secret → Render env `JWT_SECRET` | All three are in `apps/api/.env` in the working tree. Rotating JWT invalidates existing sessions — do it in a maintenance window. |
| 3 | **Set env vars in Render, not in files** | Render → `srv-d7mc2n8sfn5c73dfce0g` (API) → Environment | `NODE_ENV=production`, `DATABASE_URL`, `DIRECT_URL`, `CORS_ORIGIN=https://darshya.online,https://www.darshya.online` |
| 4 | **Decide the pandit take rate** | — | **70% (current seed) or 80% (the pitch)?** If 80: update `seed.ts:934` **and** `UPDATE priests SET commission_rate = 0.80`. Every existing pandit is on 70% and loses ₹110 per ₹1,100 booking versus what recruiting promised. |
| 5 | **Decide the one refund ladder** | — | Recommend the code's **100 / 75 / 50 / 25** with the 48h boundary — but the 24h hard block in `booking.service.ts:850` makes the 50% and 25% tiers unreachable, so also decide: keep the block (and delete those tiers) or remove it. |
| 6 | **80G: obtain or disable** | Income Tax e-filing portal → 80G(5) registration, per donee | Until the certificate number **and** the donee PAN are on file for a given gaushala, that gaushala's receipts must not say 80G. Currently `gs_001` has both `null` and receipts claim the exemption anyway. |
| 7 | **Verify production security headers** | `curl -sI https://darshya.online/auth/login \| grep -i content-security-policy` | If empty, a Cloudflare Transform Rule is **not** injecting them and the code fix in Wave 3 is mandatory. Deliberately not probed this run. |
| 8 | **Razorpay: confirm the live key and enable payouts** | Razorpay Dashboard → Settings → API Keys; then Products → **Route** and **RazorpayX** | Local `GET /payments/config` returns `{"keyId":null,"isDemo":true}`, so the gateway never opens and no end-to-end payment could be verified this run. Route/RazorpayX are what make P0-3 and P0-10 actually settle money. |
| 9 | **Incorporation, GSTIN, registered address** | MCA / GST portal → then `apps/web/lib/company.ts` | Set `legalEntity`, `cin`, `gstin`, `registeredAddress`, `IS_INCORPORATED_VERIFIED: true`. Until then, retitle the PDF from "TAX INVOICE" to "RECEIPT" and drop the 5% GST line. |
| 10 | **Name a grievance officer** | `company.ts` → new `grievanceOfficer: { name, designation, email, phone }` | Statutory under IT Rules 2021 Rule 3(2) and E-Commerce Rules 2020 Rule 4(5). Also pick **one** SLA (24h acknowledge / 15 days resolve satisfies both). |
| 11 | **CSR-1 decision** | — | Delete `/csr-match` or gate it behind `ENABLE_CSR_MATCH=false` until the CSR-1 filing and 80G(5) certificate exist. It currently makes a statutory claim to corporate donors. |
| 12 | **Launch scope: wellness and astrology** | — | Wellness has zero consumer surface; astrology sells fictional providers. **Recommend: launch pujas + donations only**, hide `/wellness` and `/astrology` from nav, and bring each back when its Wave-2/3 items land. |
| 13 | **Mobile app: confirm it is not shipping** | App Store Connect / Play Console | Six independently launch-blocking defects in a binary that cannot be hotfixed, including a free ₹5,000 subscription grant. |
| 14 | **Confirm `privacy@` and `grievance@` actually deliver** | Email/DNS provider → MX + aliases | The codebase's own comment calls these inboxes "aspirational" while two legal pages publish them as the sole rights channel. |
| 15 | **Confirm the 8 ghost `pending_payment` bookings on the demo devotee are test artefacts** | — | If any real user has these, they need cancelling and the slots releasing before launch. |

## 11. PROJECTED SCORE

### After Wave 1 — **58 / 100 · GO-WITH-CAVEATS (pujas + donations only)**

| Dimension | Now | After W1 | Why |
|---|---|---|---|
| Core flows | 7 | **15** | Puja end-to-end works: book → pay → find it → pandit accepts → delivers → gets paid. Store checkout unblocked. Astrology sells real people. Wellness deliberately hidden rather than broken. |
| Auth + roles | 7 | **8** | Astrologer demo fixed; provider-profile linkage still pending. |
| Money correctness | 6 | **11** | Payouts mint, commissions reverse, GST is consistent, revenue tile is real. Still no plan-based commission and the take-rate decision may need a backfill. |
| UI/UX | 5 | **6** | Consent overlay gone; dead filters still there. |
| Legal | 2 | **7** | The three statutory exposures (80G, refund ladder, CSR-1) removed. Entity/GSTIN/grievance still owner-gated. |
| Perf & SEO | 5 | **5** | Untouched — still the blind spot. |
| Mobile/responsive | 2 | **4** | Consent fix alone lifts this materially; tap targets pending. |
| Ops | 2 | **5** | Payouts, refunds, block/delete and a truthful revenue number. |

**58 is a real launch number for a narrow launch** — pujas and donations, invite-controlled supply, a founder watching every order. It is *not* a number for opening the store, the wellness marketplace or the app.

The caveats that persist at 58: filters and sorts on both catalogues still do nothing (the founder's original complaint); the design still reads as several products; nothing is measured on performance; and the mobile app stays off the stores.

| Dimension | After W1 | After W2 |
|---|---|---|
| Core flows | 15 | **19** |
| Auth + roles | 8 | **9** |
| Money correctness | 11 | **13** |
| UI/UX | 6 | **10** |
| Legal | 7 | **8** |
| Perf & SEO | 5 | **6** |
| Mobile/responsive | 4 | **4** |
| Ops | 5 | **7** |

**74 is the same number the prior code-based audit produced — and this time it would be earned by clicking.** That symmetry is the honest summary of this whole report: the prior audit was measuring a product that was 74% *written*. Wave 1 and Wave 2 are the work of making it 74% *wired*.

The 26 points still missing at that stage are the deliberate Wave-3 scope: the wellness demand side, the mobile app, URL-addressable facet pages and the SEO work behind them, the full design-token migration, and a performance budget that nobody has measured yet. None of those block revenue; all of them cap it.


==========================================================================================
# WORKFLOW: scale-mindset  (10 agents)
==========================================================================================

## AREA: customer-mindset   score=34

### [CM-01] P0/ux-mindset/S Every temple shows a 0.2-star rating on the puja detail page (trustScore is 0-5, divided by 20)
  - where: apps/web/app/pujas/[slug]/page.tsx:501-503 — rendered on http://127.0.0.1:3000/pujas/rudrabhishek
  - evidence: Source: `<Star className="h-3 w-3 fill-amber-500 text-amber-500" />{(temple.trustScore / 20).toFixed(1)}`. Postgres darshya_utf8 `select name, trust_score from temples order by trust_score desc`: Tirumala Tirupati Devasthanam=5, Kashi Vishwanath=4.9, Somnath=4.9, Jagannath=4.9 … Lingaraj=4.7, Birla Mandir Hyderabad=4.6. min 4.6 / max 5.0 across all 20 seeded temples. Rendered value = 4.9/20 = 0.2. Live page text captured from /pujas/rudrabhishek: "Kashi Vishwanath Temple / Varanasi, Uttar Pradesh / 0.2 / 7 time slots / ₹1,100 / Book Now" and "Somnath Temple / Somnath, Gujarat / 0.2 / 7 time slots / ₹1,100 / Book Now". The divisor implies a 0-100 scale; three other call sites agree it is 0-100 (apps/web/app/pujas/[slug]/temples/page.tsx:163 prints "/100", apps/web/app/pujas/book/page.tsx:1446 carries the comment "trustScore is 0-100", apps/web/app/temples/page.tsx:76 gates a Jyotirlinga badge on `trustScore >= 97`). The database disagrees with all four.
  - customer_impact: A first-time devotee arrives at the exact decision screen — two temples, same ₹1,100 price, which one do I trust with my Rudrabhishek — and the only comparison signal on the row is a gold star next to "0.2". Read on any 5-point convention that is a near-zero rating for Kashi Vishwanath, one of the most revered shrines in Hinduism. The reaction is not "the number is broken", it is "this site is fake". It fires on 100% of puja detail sessions and on every temple row.
  - fix: trustScore is a Float 0-5 in Postgres (prisma schema.prisma:290 `trustScore Float @default(0)`). Delete the `/20` and render `{temple.trustScore.toFixed(1)}` with an explicit "/5" suffix, or better, stop labelling an internal ops score with a star glyph at all — a star means customer reviews to every shopper alive. Pick one scale, assert it in a shared type or Zod schema at the API boundary, and fix the four disagreeing call sites together (pujas/[slug]:502, pujas/[slug]/temples:163, pujas/book:1449, temples/page:76 — the last two currently silently hide their badges because 4.9 fails their `>= 20` and `>= 97` guards).

### [CM-02] P0/ux-mindset/S Temple comparison page literally prints "Trust score: 4.9/100" under copy that says "Higher = safer"
  - where: apps/web/app/pujas/[slug]/temples/page.tsx:163 — http://127.0.0.1:3000/pujas/rudrabhishek/temples
  - evidence: Verbatim page text read from the live page: "VARANASI, UTTAR PRADESH / ✓ Verified / Kashi Vishwanath Temple / Trust score: 4.9/100 / ₹1,100 / View temple →" and "Somnath Temple / Trust score: 4.9/100". Immediately below, in the same viewport: "Not sure which temple? Trust score combines temple verification, devotee reviews, on-time delivery and video-proof completion. Higher = safer." Source line: `<p className="text-xs text-surface-500 mb-3">Trust score: {o.trustScore}/100</p>`. DB value is 4.9 on a 0-5 scale.
  - customer_impact: This page exists solely to answer "which temple should I give my money to", it is linked from the detail page as "Compare all →", and it teaches the user the scale in one sentence and then violates it in the next. A buyer reads 4.9 out of 100 for both options and concludes both temples are 95% untrustworthy by the platform's own admission. Worse than showing nothing.
  - fix: Same root cause as CM-01. Render `{o.trustScore.toFixed(1)}/5`, or replace the raw score with the plain-language components the explainer already promises ("Verified · 7 slots · video proof on 100% of bookings") — those are concrete and do not depend on a scale the customer has to learn.

### [CM-03] P0/ux-mindset/S Advertised ₹1,100 becomes ₹1,301 at step 3 of 4, on a site that promises "₹0 hidden fees"
  - where: apps/web/app/pujas/book/page.tsx:141-142 (fee constants), 594-597 (total), 2098-2129 (breakdown UI); claim at apps/web/components/home/TrustStrip.tsx
  - evidence: `const PLATFORM_FEE = 51; const PRASAD_DELIVERY_FEE = 150;` and `let total = basePrice + PLATFORM_FEE; if (wantsPrasad) total += PRASAD_DELIVERY_FEE;`. The Price Breakdown block that first discloses these renders inside STEP 3 of a 4-step wizard (STEP_LABELS = ['Temple & Date','Devotee Details','Review & Prasad','Payment']). Every prior surface says ₹1,100: homepage "Popular Puja — Rudrabhishek at Kashi, ₹1,100", /pujas card "₹1,100 onwards", detail hero "Starting from ₹1,100", detail sidebar "Price (INR) — ₹1,100 onwards", temple compare "₹1,100". Homepage trust strip reads verbatim "₹0 hidden fees". Base ₹1,100 + ₹51 + ₹150 = ₹1,301 = +18.3%; without prasad ₹1,151 = +4.6%.
  - customer_impact: Price shock at the last gate is the single best-documented cause of checkout abandonment, and here it is compounded by an explicit contradicting promise the buyer read on the homepage. The ₹150 is not an optional extra either — "Prasad Delivery / Sacred prasad to your door" is one of only four trust pillars merchandised on every puja page, so the buyer has been told for four screens that prasad is part of the offer and then billed for it. An Indian buyer who has been conditioned by IRCTC/Swiggy convenience-fee tactics will read this as exactly that.
  - fix: Either fold ₹51 into basePrice and delete the line (cleanest — it is 4.6%, not a real pass-through), or surface "₹1,100 + ₹51 platform fee" on the card, the detail hero and the temple compare row so the number never changes. Show the prasad toggle with its ₹150 at step 1 next to the trust pillar that promises it, not at step 3. And remove "₹0 hidden fees" from TrustStrip until the funnel actually has none.

### [CM-04] P0/comms/M Three contradictory refund policies are live simultaneously; the most prominent one is a hardcoded badge the written policy does not honour
  - where: Badge: apps/web/app/pujas/[slug]/page.tsx:382 · Published policy: http://127.0.0.1:3000/refunds · Enforced: apps/api/src/services/cancellation.service.ts:47-53 · Checkbox: apps/web/app/pujas/book/page.tsx:2151
  - evidence: (1) Puja detail trust pillar, hardcoded in a literal array: `{ icon: Undo2, label: 'Money-Back Guarantee', desc: '100% refund if not satisfied' }`. (2) /refunds page table, read live: ">24h = 100% refund · 6 to 24 hours = 50% refund · <6 hours = No refund (preparations underway)". (3) DEFAULT_CANCEL_POLICY actually enforced: `{48h,1.0} {24h,0.75} {4h,0.5} {0h,0.25}`. (4) Booking terms checkbox: "I understand that cancellation is allowed up to 24 hours before the scheduled puja time." Worked example, cancel 12h out: badge promises 100%, policy page says 50%, code pays 75%, checkbox implies cancellation is not even permitted. Cancel 3h out: badge 100%, policy 0%, code 25%. "Not satisfied" is not a refund ground anywhere in the written policy.
  - customer_impact: The badge is the single loudest trust signal on the product page and it is the one that is false. Every buyer who cancels or complains discovers the gap at their angriest moment, which converts into support load, Razorpay chargebacks and one-star reviews — the exact reputational damage this category cannot survive. In India it is also a Consumer Protection (E-Commerce) Rules 2020 exposure: the displayed cancellation/refund terms must match the terms actually applied.
  - fix: Make DEFAULT_CANCEL_POLICY the single source of truth and render the badge and the /refunds table from it, not from literals. Change the badge to the strongest claim you can actually keep — "Free cancellation up to 48h · full refund if the puja isn't performed or video proof doesn't arrive in 48h" is both true per the policy page and stronger than a vague money-back guarantee. Update the checkbox copy to match, and put the cancellation line as microcopy directly under the Book button (see CM-13).

### [CM-05] P0/ux-mindset/M Zero social proof anywhere in the funnel, while 39 real bookings and 6 uploaded proof videos sit unused in the database
  - where: http://127.0.0.1:3000/ , /pujas , /pujas/rudrabhishek ; API GET /api/v1/pujas/types ; tables reviews, service_ratings, puja_bookings
  - evidence: Full homepage innerText (6,966 chars) probed for social-proof tokens: "★"=0, "⭐"=0, "testimonial"=0, "rating"=0, "review"=1 (a footer link), "devotees"=1 (the "1,000 founding devotees" early-access chip). Same on /pujas and /pujas/rudrabhishek — no star glyph, no review count, no "N booked". The API contract cannot supply it: GET /api/v1/pujas/types returns exactly {id,name,nameLocal,slug,description,category,deity,durationMinutes,basePrice,nriPrice,samagriIncluded,images,sortOrder} — no rating, reviewCount, bookingCount or templeCount field exists. Meanwhile Postgres has: `reviews` = 0 rows, `service_ratings` = 0 rows, but `puja_bookings` = 34 rows (Rudrabhishek 39 bookings, Ganesh Puja 5) with statuses priest_assigned=22, video_uploaded=6, delivered=3, completed=1, and columns `video_proof_url` + `video_uploaded_at` already populated on the video_uploaded rows.
  - customer_impact: This is a prepay-a-stranger-₹1,100-for-an-unwitnessable-service category. Trust is not a feature here, it is the product. The site asserts trustworthiness eleven times ("verified" x7, "video proof" x4) and demonstrates it zero times. A buyer comparing against Sri Mandir or VAMA sees their review counts and Darshya's blank space and leaves. The cruelty is that the proof already exists — six completed pujas with proof videos on disk and nobody is shown one.
  - fix: Three moves, cheapest first. (1) Add `bookingCount` and `completedCount` to the /pujas/types list and detail contracts and render "39 devotees booked this puja" on the card and hero — no new data collection needed, it is a COUNT on puja_bookings. (2) Embed one real proof video (a video_proof_url from a video_uploaded booking, with consent) on the detail page under a "See what you receive" heading — "video proof" is claimed 4x on the homepage and never shown once. (3) Trigger a review request on the `delivered` status transition and surface aggregate stars once n>=5 per puja; the `reviews` and `service_ratings` tables and POST /ratings already exist, nothing writes to them from the puja path.

### [CM-06] P0/i18n/M NRI cannot complete a booking: multi-currency is advertised but never delivered, and non-Indian postcode/phone are rejected at both web and API
  - where: Web validation apps/web/app/pujas/book/page.tsx:711-717, 2070-2083 · API schema apps/api/src/routes/bookings.ts:1825-1835 · Claims on http://127.0.0.1:3000/ and /gift
  - evidence: Web: `if (!/^\d{6}$/.test(address.pincode.trim())) errs.addr_pincode = 'Enter valid 6-digit pincode'` and `if (!/^[6-9]\d{9}$/.test(address.phone.trim())) errs.addr_phone = 'Enter valid 10-digit phone number'`. Grep for country|Country|Postal|ZIP across the entire 2,341-line booking page returns only the pincode lines — there is no country field. API mirrors it: `pincode: z.string().regex(/^\d{6}$/), phone: z.string().regex(/^[6-9]\d{9}$/)`. Homepage claim, verbatim: "Gift a puja from abroad — INR or USD, GBP, AUD, CAD" and trust strip "NRI Friendly / Book from 40+ countries". Reality per /gift page text: "Billing is in INR; international cards are accepted." API comment at bookings.ts:1819-1823: the currency enum "never changes the amount and never changes the gateway currency — `const currency = 'INR'` below is unconditional". A US ZIP (07030, 5 digits), a UK postcode (SW1A 1AA), a US mobile (+1 201 555 0123) each fail both layers.
  - customer_impact: NRI is the cohort the whole pricing doctrine is built on (schema comment: "business rule #2: 9-10x INR", nriPrice stored at 9,999 against a 1,100 base). That buyer is told on the homepage they can pay in their own currency and ship prasad home, then hits a form that will not accept their address or their phone and a gateway that only speaks INR. They do not file a bug, they close the tab. Every foreign-currency word on the homepage is a promise the checkout cannot keep.
  - fix: Add a Country select defaulting to India; branch postcode/phone validation on it (libphonenumber-js for phone, permissive alphanumeric 3-10 for postcode outside IN) in both the web form and the Zod schema — they must change together or the API rejects what the form accepted. Until the PayPal/multi-currency rail actually ships, delete "USD, GBP, AUD, CAD" from the homepage strip and say what is true: "Book from anywhere — billed in INR, international cards accepted." That sentence already exists on /gift and costs nothing to reuse.

### [CM-07] P1/ux-mindset/M /gift, the NRI conversion page, has no prices and no checkout — it is a lead form with a 4-hour reply SLA
  - where: http://127.0.0.1:3000/gift
  - evidence: Page text read in full: step 3 of its own How-It-Works is "We reply with a quote — Our team replies within 4 hours with pandit and date options, the final amount, and a secure payment link." Form CTA: "We'll email you within 4 hours with available pandits + dates." Regex scan of the full page text for `(₹|Rs\.?|\$|USD|GBP|£|€)\s?[\d,]+` returned zero matches — not one price anywhere on the page. The puja picker lists 9 named pujas plus "Not sure — recommend one", and 7 occasions, all without prices.
  - customer_impact: The highest-intent, highest-value visitor on the site — an NRI at 11pm in New Jersey who just remembered their mother's anniversary — is asked to submit a form and wait four hours for a number. Purchase intent in this category is emotional and time-boxed; a four-hour gap loses most of it, and the buyer has no idea whether this costs ₹2,000 or ₹20,000 before committing their details. Meanwhile the self-serve /pujas flow next door does have prices and does take payment, so the concierge path is strictly worse for the customer who is already sold.
  - fix: Show indicative prices on the picker immediately ("Satyanarayan Puja from ₹1,501") — the data is in puja_types and already public on /pujas. Offer the self-serve rail as the default with the concierge form as the fallback for "Not sure — recommend one", rather than routing everyone through a queue. If the 4-hour SLA must stay, state the hours it applies in (IST) so an overseas buyer can calibrate.

### [CM-08] P1/scalability/M Product cards ship 5.58 MB of unoptimised hotlinked Wikimedia images, using 19th-century paintings as product photography
  - where: apps/web/app/pujas/page.tsx:573-588 — http://127.0.0.1:3000/pujas
  - evidence: Card markup is a raw `<img src={puja.imageUrl ?? getDeityImage(...)} loading="lazy" className="absolute inset-0 w-full h-full object-cover" />` inside `aspect-[16/10]` — not next/image, no width/height, no onError. currentSrc on all 10 cards is the raw upload.wikimedia.org original (no /_next/image/ proxy). HEAD on the 10 image URLs from GET /api/v1/pujas/types: Ram Charit Manas Path 3,592 KB PNG, Lakshmi Puja 1,313 KB, Ganesh Puja 429 KB, Satyanarayan Katha 251 KB, Navgraha 121 KB, Maha Mrityunjay 3 KB SVG — total 5.58 MB for a 10-card grid whose slots measure 382x239 CSS px. A single browser fetch of the Rudrabhishek image measured 2,218 KB / 4,757 ms with naturalWidth 2592. Four of the ten HEADs returned 429 when fired as a 10-way concurrent burst with a generic UA; each returned 200 when retried individually with a browser UA and Referer, so the 429s are Wikimedia burst-throttling rather than a permanent block — but a real page load is also a 10-way concurrent burst. Content of the assets: "Ganesha Basohli miniature circa 1730", "Rāmacaritamānasa manuscript (19th century)", "1_Om.svg" (a generic Om glyph, for a ₹2,501 Maha Mrityunjay Jaap).
  - customer_impact: Two separate injuries. Commercially: the hero image on a ₹3,501 product is a scan of a 19th-century manuscript and on a ₹2,501 product it is a clip-art Om — neither shows a priest, a temple, or the ritual, so the card gives the buyer nothing to want. Operationally: the catalogue's images live on a third-party host that documents burst rate-limiting and can serve a 429 at any time, and with no onError fallback the card degrades to a blank 382x239 box. On an Indian 4G connection 5.58 MB of thumbnails is a multi-second grey grid on the main shopping page.
  - fix: Self-host the catalogue images (or push them through a CDN/Cloudinary), resize to ~2x the 382x239 slot, serve WebP/AVIF, and switch to next/image with explicit width/height so the aspect box does not shift. Add an onError fallback to the deity gradient already behind the img. Longer term this is a merchandising problem, not just a bytes problem: commission or capture real photography of the actual priests and sanctums — that is also the cheapest available trust signal (see CM-05). Note this overlaps known issues #203/#204 in TASKS.md, which record 13/15 homepage images broken and 11-15 MB pages on production.

### [CM-09] P1/ux-mindset/S The only clickable region on a product card is an 89x34px button, it is a plain <a> not a Link, and "Book Now" does not go to booking
  - where: apps/web/app/pujas/page.tsx:568 (card container), 651-657 (the anchor) — http://127.0.0.1:3000/pujas
  - evidence: Card container is `<div key={puja.id} className="group bg-... card-hover ...">` with no Link wrapper — the image, the title and the price are inert. The only interactive element is `<a href={`/pujas/${puja.slug}`} ...>Book Now</a>`: a bare `<a>`, so every card click is a full document navigation that discards the Next client cache and re-downloads the app shell. Measured on a 390x844 mobile viewport, every one of the 10 "Book Now" controls is 89x34 px (WCAG 2.5.5 / Apple HIG minimum is 44x44); 54 sub-44px tap targets on /pujas overall, 62 on the homepage, including the two hero CTAs "Explore Temples" 155x38 and "Book a Puja" 108x38. Destination check: clicking "Book Now" from /pujas lands on http://127.0.0.1:3000/pujas/rudrabhishek — the content page, not a booking form.
  - customer_impact: Shoppers tap product images. Here the image is dead and the live target is a 34px-tall button — on a phone that is a miss-and-retry, and the fat-finger miss lands on nothing at all rather than on the product. The label compounds it: "Book Now" sets the expectation of a form and delivers an article, so the user pays an unexpected navigation and then has to find the real Book control further down. Every one of those hops is a full page reload.
  - fix: Wrap the whole card in a next/link `<Link href={`/pujas/${slug}`}>` so image, title and price are all targets and navigation is client-side; keep the button as a visual affordance inside it. Raise the button to min-height 44px on mobile. Rename it "View details" (honest) or make it deep-link straight to `/pujas/{slug}/book` (fast) — either is better than promising booking and delivering reading.

### [CM-10] P1/comms/S Card and hero copy is a 1,144-character scripture excerpt; raw enum values like "special_occasion" are shown to customers
  - where: apps/web/app/pujas/page.tsx:629-639 (card), apps/web/app/pujas/[slug]/page.tsx:296-298 (hero) — /pujas and /pujas/rudrabhishek
  - evidence: GET /api/v1/pujas/types: descriptions average 1,144 chars and are 11,436 of the 15,767-byte payload (72.5%). The card renders that field under `line-clamp-2` (~110 visible chars = 9.6% of what was shipped), producing card copy like "Rudrabhishek is the ceremonial bathing of a Shiva linga performed while reciting the Shri Rudram and Chamakam …" and "Satyanarayan Katha is the recitation of five chapters from the Skanda Purana, Reva Khanda, in which Sage Suta …". The detail hero has no clamp at all — `<p className="mb-6 max-w-xl ...">{puja.description}</p>` — so the full 1,144 chars sit between the H1 and the price card. Category is printed raw: page text scan of /pujas found the literal strings "planetary" (4x), "special_occasion" (4x), "ancestral" (2x), "prosperity" (2x), "health" (1x) in the card metadata row.
  - customer_impact: A product card has about two seconds to make someone want the thing. These two seconds are spent on Taittiriya Samhita citations. Nothing in the visible 110 characters tells the buyer what they get, who performs it, how long it takes or why they would choose this over the card next to it. On the detail page the wall of text pushes the price and the Book button far down — on a 390px phone 1,144 characters is roughly 900px of prose before the buyer reaches a price. And "special_occasion" with an underscore is a database column leaking onto a consumer storefront; it reads as unfinished software at the exact moment the buyer is deciding whether these people can be trusted with money.
  - fix: Add a `shortDescription` (or `cardBlurb`) field, 100-140 chars, benefit-first — "90-minute Shiva abhishek at Kashi Vishwanath. Samagri included, live video, prasad to your door." Return it in the list contract and drop `description` from the list response entirely (that alone removes 72.5% of the payload). Clamp the detail hero to ~2 lines with the full text staying in the existing "About This Puja" section below, where a reader who wants the Shiva Purana citations will find them. Map the PujaCategory enum to display labels ("Planetary remedies", "Special occasions", "Ancestral rites").

### [CM-11] P1/comms/S Homepage trust numbers "200+ verified temples" and "500+ vetted pandits" are hardcoded strings, not counts
  - where: apps/web/components/home/TrustStrip.tsx:9-10, also components/home/WorshipStrip.tsx:20, components/home/IntentTrio.tsx:23, app/waitlist/page.tsx:57
  - evidence: `{ Icon: Shield, text: '200+ verified temples' }, { Icon: Users, text: '500+ vetted pandits' }` — string literals, no data source. "Watch live aarti from 200+ temples" is repeated as a literal in two more components and on /waitlist. These four claims plus "₹0 hidden fees" and "80G receipt available" are the entire quantitative trust content of the homepage. Seeded reality in this environment: 20 temples, 3 priests.
  - customer_impact: Because the strings cannot track inventory, they can only be right by coincidence. A sceptical devotee — the one you most need to convert — clicks through to /temples to verify, counts the listings, and finds an order of magnitude less than claimed. That single check destroys more trust than the claim ever built, and it is the exact check a careful buyer performs before a first ₹1,100 purchase. It is also a live ASCI / Consumer Protection Act misleading-advertisement exposure once the site takes real money.
  - fix: Drive the strip from a cached count endpoint (`SELECT count(*) FROM temples WHERE is_verified`) and render the true number — "20 verified temples, every one manager-signed" is a stronger claim than an unverifiable 200+, because it invites the check instead of failing it. If the marketing number must stay ahead of inventory, phrase it as a roadmap ("200 temples by March") rather than as a present-tense fact.

### [CM-12] P1/ux-mindset/M Site search only searches encyclopedia articles — a devotee searching "rudrabhishek" cannot find the puja they can buy
  - where: http://127.0.0.1:3000/search — header search box on every page
  - evidence: H1 on /search is "Search knowledge", subtitle "Across Sanskrit, Vedas, and Sanatan Dharma articles." Page title "Search Knowledge — Darshya". Empty state for /search?q=zzzqqqnothing reads in full: "0 results for “zzzqqqnothing” / No matches. Try a different term." — no suggestions, no popular searches, no link to /pujas or /temples. The header input is the only search affordance on the site and its placeholder rotates through "Search festivals + muhurat…", "Search puja, temple, kundli, saint…", "Find a tirth or pilgrimage…", "Find your kundli…", "Look up a saint…" — three of those five explicitly promise puja and temple results the destination does not return.
  - customer_impact: Search is where high-intent users go. Someone who already knows they want Rudrabhishek types it into the box that says "Search puja, temple, kundli, saint…" and gets scripture articles, or nothing. The placeholder actively misleads about scope. And the zero-result state is a hard dead end — no route back into the catalogue at the moment the user has proven both intent and a willingness to type.
  - fix: Make the header search federated: pujas, temples, store products and articles in one result set, grouped, with pujas first — the catalogue is small enough that even a naive multi-query merge is fine. Until then, at minimum align the placeholder to what the page actually searches, and add "Browse all pujas / Browse temples" recovery links to the zero-result state.

### [CM-13] P1/ux-mindset/M Homepage is brand-first with two competing CTAs, no how-it-works, no proof video, and a cookie banner over 29% of the fold
  - where: http://127.0.0.1:3000/
  - evidence: H1 measured at y=213: "Darshya, your bridge to sacred traditions" — a brand line; the product definition is demoted to the subhead "Live temple darshan. Authentic pujas with verified priests and video proof." Two equally-weighted hero buttons at the same y: "Explore Temples" and "Book a Puja". Cookie banner measured top=512, height=208 on a 1280x720 viewport = 29% of the fold, present on first paint, three buttons. Page scrollHeight 8,222px desktop (11.4 screens) / 12,151px on 390x844 mobile (14.4 screens), 15 content H2 sections and 43 H3s. Section audit shows Live Darshan merchandised four separate times (y=629 card, y=1504 grid, y=1865 full section, y=4543 "WORSHIP" block) and "Book a Puja" appearing as a CTA five times. Token scan of the full 6,966-char page text: "How it works" = 0 occurrences, "video proof" = 4 assertions with 0 examples shown, "refund" = 1 (footer link only), "guarantee" = 0. The strongest trust line on the page, "₹0 hidden fees", sits at y≈7,000 — ten screens below the fold, and is contradicted by CM-03.
  - customer_impact: In the first 30 seconds a first-timer learns the brand name and a metaphor, not what they can buy. Two co-equal CTAs split intent (Hick's Law at the highest-stakes decision point), and "Explore Temples" is the weaker branch — see CM-14. Then 11 screens of the same four propositions restated, with no answer to the one question every buyer in this category has: "how do I know the puja actually happened?" The proof mechanism is asserted four times and demonstrated zero times, and the refund terms that would de-risk a first purchase are a footer link.
  - fix: Rewrite the H1 to state the job — "Book a puja at India's most revered temples. Verified priests, video proof, prasad to your door." Demote "Explore Temples" to a text link and make "Book a Puja" the single primary. Insert a 3-step How-It-Works with a real 20-second proof-video clip directly under the hero (the asset exists — 6 bookings carry video_proof_url). Lift "Free cancellation up to 48h" next to the CTA. Delay the cookie banner until first scroll or a 3s dwell so it is not the first thing read. Cut the page to 5-6 sections by deleting the three duplicate Live Darshan blocks.

### [CM-14] P1/ux-mindset/S 75% of temples have nothing bookable, yet "Explore Temples" is a co-primary homepage CTA
  - where: http://127.0.0.1:3000/temples and its detail pages; tables temples, temple_puja_offerings
  - evidence: Postgres: 20 temples, 25 offerings total, and `SELECT count(*) FROM temples t WHERE NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM temple_puja_offerings o WHERE o.temple_id = t.id)` = 15. The 15 with zero offerings include the highest-trust-score names in the seed: Vaishno Devi Bhawan, Shirdi Sai Baba, Badrinath, Meenakshi Amman, Akshardham Delhi, Dwarkadhish, ISKCON Bangalore, Dagdusheth Pune. Per-puja depth is also thin: Rudrabhishek is offered by 2 temples, Pitru Dosh Nivaran by 1, the best-covered (Navgraha Shanti) by 4. The /pujas/rudrabhishek/temples page states it plainly: "2 verified temples offer Rudrabhishek."
  - customer_impact: Half the hero traffic is pointed at a directory where three of every four destinations cannot take money. A devotee who came specifically for Vaishno Devi or Shirdi — two of the highest-demand shrines in India — lands on a beautifully written history page with no booking. That is a worse outcome than not listing them, because the user now believes the platform does not serve their temple. The 1-4 temples per puja also removes the comparison shopping the /temples/compare page is built for.
  - fix: Short term, make the state legible instead of silent: badge unbookable temples "Darshan info only — pujas coming soon" in the directory, and put a "Notify me when pujas open here" capture on those detail pages so the demand is measured rather than lost. Re-point the hero's second CTA at "Browse pujas" until coverage is real. Medium term this is a supply problem — coverage per high-demand shrine is the number to manage, not total temple count.

### [CM-15] P2/ux-mindset/S /temples and /astrology render an empty full-viewport div as their loading state
  - where: apps/web/app/temples/page.tsx:125 and apps/web/app/astrology/page.tsx:170
  - evidence: Both are `<Suspense fallback={<div className="min-h-[100dvh]" />}>` — an empty div one viewport tall, no skeleton, no spinner, no text. Confirmed in the served HTML for /temples: `<main ...><!--$?--><template id="B:1"></template><div class="min-h-[100dvh]"></div><!--/$-->…</main>` — the streamed payload does contain the temple names (Kashi Vishwanath, Somnath, Tirumala, Siddhivinayak, Meenakshi all present in the 413,983-byte response), but until the boundary resolves the user sees header, a full screen of white, then footer. Reproduced in a live browser session where the boundary had not resolved: get_page_text on /temples returned only the header nav and the footer, with nothing between them.
  - customer_impact: On a slow connection or a cold API the temple directory — one of two hero destinations — presents as a blank page. Users do not wait through blank; they read it as broken and leave. A skeleton would hold them for the same duration because it signals that something is coming.
  - fix: Replace both fallbacks with a card-grid skeleton matching the real layout (6-9 shimmer cards). This is the standard Next pattern and takes one small component reused in both places.

### [CM-16] P2/ux-mindset/S Filter-driven empty state blames the platform's inventory and offers no way out
  - where: apps/web/app/pujas/page.tsx:543-562
  - evidence: The empty block branches on `query` only. With a text query it is good: "No pujas match “x” / Try a different ritual name, deity, or category — or browse the full catalogue." plus a "Browse all pujas" button. With filters and no query — e.g. category=Abhishekam plus the "Under ₹500" price band, both offered in the UI — `query` is falsy, so the copy becomes "No pujas available right now / Please check back shortly." and the recovery Link is not rendered at all (`{query && (<Link …>)}`). The page offers 8 categories, 10 deities, 9 occasions and 5 price bands over a catalogue of 10 items, so empty combinations are the common case, not the edge case.
  - customer_impact: The user narrowed a filter and is told the platform has nothing — which is false and reads as "this site is empty, come back later". The true message is "your filters are too narrow". And with no clear-filters control and no back-out link, the only escape is the browser back button or re-editing filters one by one.
  - fix: Branch on "any filter active" rather than on `query`: "No pujas match these filters" plus a "Clear all filters" button, always render the "Browse all pujas" link, and show the 2-3 nearest matches (same deity, next price band). Given a 10-item catalogue, also consider disabling filter options that would yield zero results rather than letting the user walk into a dead end.

### [CM-17] P2/ux-mindset/S 404 page is a dead end with a single "Back to Home" link
  - where: apps/web/app/not-found.tsx:60-66
  - evidence: The entire interactive content of the 404 is one `<Link href="/">Back to Home</Link>`. No search box, no popular pujas, no suggested temples. Confirmed live: GET /pujas/this-puja-does-not-exist and /temples/no-such-temple-xyz both return 404 with this page. This path is known to be trafficked — TASKS.md records issue #202, 19 of 20 temple sitemap URLs 404ing on production before a fix.
  - customer_impact: WhatsApp forwards, old bookmarks and stale search results are a primary acquisition channel for this audience. A devotee who taps a shared Rudrabhishek link that has since changed slug is dumped at square one and has to re-navigate an 8,222px homepage to find what they were sent. The intent was already qualified and it is thrown away.
  - fix: Add a search input and a "Popular pujas" / "Popular temples" row (3-4 items each, statically generated) to the 404, and where the failed path is parseable (/pujas/<something>) offer the closest slug match. Cheap, and it recycles traffic you already paid for.

### [CM-18] P2/ux-mindset/S Save and Share on the puja price card are dead buttons — the detail page contains zero click handlers
  - where: apps/web/app/pujas/[slug]/page.tsx:357-367
  - evidence: `<button className="flex items-center gap-1 text-sm text-white/80 hover:text-white"><Heart …/>Save</button>` and the sibling Share button carry no onClick. Grep for `onClick` across the whole 621-line file returns no matches. Both render with hover styling, so they signal interactivity and deliver none.
  - customer_impact: Save is the natural micro-conversion for the large fraction of buyers who are not ready to pay today — a puja is often booked against a date (a shraadh, an anniversary, a muhurat) that is weeks away. Offering the affordance and doing nothing loses that user entirely rather than capturing them into a wishlist you could remind. Share is the organic-growth loop in a WhatsApp-first audience, also silently discarded. A user who clicks twice and sees nothing happen also downgrades their estimate of how finished the product is.
  - fix: Wire Share to `navigator.share` with a clipboard fallback (a few lines, and it is the highest-leverage of the two here). For Save, either persist to a wishlist for signed-in users and prompt sign-in otherwise, or remove the button until it works — a missing feature costs less trust than a broken one.

### [CM-19] P2/comms/S /pricing advertises a 30% provider commission against the shipped 20% uniform take rate
  - where: http://127.0.0.1:3000/pricing
  - evidence: Page text: "Free — Get started with a basic listing. No upfront cost. / 30 % commission rate / … / 30% platform commission". The repo's own marketplace doctrine, recorded in TASKS.md under the #233 provider-payouts work, is "Take-rate doctrine: uniform 20% platform / 80% provider across all roles", and the astrologer revamp (#227) explicitly moved 30% to 20%.
  - customer_impact: Supply-side, not devotee-side, but it is a public page a prospective pandit or temple manager reads before signing up — and Darshya's competitive pitch to them is precisely that its take rate beats AstroTalk's 50%. Publishing 30% throws away that advantage and, for anyone who signs at 30% and is then charged 20%, makes the pricing page look like it was written by a different company than the one running the ledger.
  - fix: Update /pricing to the actual 20% and derive the tier table from the same constant the commission math uses, so the two cannot drift again. Worth a quick sweep for other pages quoting a commission number.

### [CM-20] P2/a11y/S Primary CTAs and every Book Now fall below the 44px minimum tap target on mobile
  - where: http://127.0.0.1:3000/ and /pujas measured at 390x844
  - evidence: Measured bounding boxes at a 390x844 viewport. Homepage: 62 anchors/buttons under 44px tall, including the two hero CTAs "Explore Temples" 155x38 and "Book a Puja" 108x38, "Book parvati puja" 158x34, "Learn more" 113x34, and "All streams" / "Browse Pujas" at 15px tall. /pujas: 54 under 44px, including all ten "Book Now" buttons at 89x34. WCAG 2.1 SC 2.5.5 (AAA) and 2.5.8 (AA, 24px) plus Apple HIG and Material both put the floor at 44-48px. No horizontal overflow on either page (scrollWidth == 390), so the layout itself is sound. Page lengths at this viewport: home 12,151px (14.4 screens), /pujas 8,045px (9.5 screens for 10 products).
  - customer_impact: This is a mobile-first Indian audience, often on a phone, often older devotees. Every conversion button on the site is 34-38px, which produces missed taps, and on /pujas a missed tap lands on dead card space (CM-09) so nothing happens at all. The 15px "All streams" and "Browse Pujas" links are effectively untappable. Compounding it, a 14.4-screen homepage means most of these controls are reached only after a long scroll.
  - fix: Set a global minimum of 44px height (and 44px hit area via padding, not just visual size) on all anchor/button components; that is a token change in the shared button styles plus a sweep of the ad-hoc inline ones. Re-measure with the same bounding-box audit after.

## AREA: scalability   score=34

### [SCAL-01] P0/scalability/S Rate limiter is a single global 100 req/min bucket for the entire platform
  - where: apps/api/src/server.ts:203-220 (rate-limit registration + keyGenerator), apps/api/src/server.ts:115 (Fastify({logger}) — no trustProxy), apps/api/src/middlewares/auth.ts:85 (authenticateUser is a preHandler)
  - evidence: Measured against the live local API (Playwright, one minute window): 96 authenticated GET /api/v1/users/me as devotee@demo.com -> {"200":87,"429":9}. Immediately after, 6 requests as a DIFFERENT authenticated user (pandit@demo.com) -> {"429":6}. Then 6 anonymous GET /api/v1/temples each with a distinct X-Forwarded-For (203.0.113.1-6) and X-Real-IP -> {"429":6}. GET /health during the throttle -> 200. Two independent defects produce this: (a) `keyGenerator: (req) => { const uid = req.currentUser?.id; return uid ? `u:${uid}` : `ip:${req.ip}` }` runs in @fastify/rate-limit's onRequest hook, but `authenticateUser` that sets `request.currentUser` is registered as a preHandler and runs later — so the `u:<id>` branch is unreachable dead code and every request keys on IP; (b) the Fastify instance is constructed as `Fastify({ logger: {...} })` with no `trustProxy`, so `req.ip` is the socket peer, not the client — confirmed by the XFF test above. Compounding both: a separate Playwright run instrumenting page.on('request') recorded 0 browser-originated /api/v1 calls on /, /temples, /pujas and /dashboard — the pages are server-rendered, so the API is called from the darshya-web container, one process with one IP. Route-level overrides make it worse in places: apps/api/src/routes/ai.ts:11 is max 20/min and apps/api/src/routes/gift-puja.ts:149 is max 2 per HOUR, also shared globally.
  - customer_impact: Above roughly 1.7 requests/second across all of Darshya, real devotees get "Rate limit exceeded, retry in N seconds" — during Navratri, a temple's WhatsApp blast, or any press mention, the site simply stops working for everyone at once. Because /health is on the allowList and keeps returning 200, Render's health check stays green and nothing pages you. The gift-puja route at 2/hour means the second person on the whole platform to gift a puja in an hour is refused.
  - fix: Three changes in apps/api/src/server.ts. (1) `Fastify({ logger: {...}, trustProxy: true })` so req.ip resolves from X-Forwarded-For behind Render's router and Cloudflare. (2) Make the user key actually reachable: either register @fastify/rate-limit with `hook: 'preHandler'` so it runs after authenticateUser, or decode the JWT `sub` cheaply inside keyGenerator itself rather than reading request.currentUser. (3) Raise RATE_LIMIT_MAX well above 100 for the per-IP anonymous bucket (600/min is still abusive-traffic protection) and add an allowList entry — or a much higher dedicated bucket — for the darshya-web container's server-to-server SSR calls, which are your own traffic and should never be throttled as if one abusive client. Add a test that asserts two different users do not share a bucket.

### [SCAL-02] P0/scalability/M API runs in Render Oregon while the database is Supabase Tokyo — every query crosses the Pacific
  - where: render.yaml (`region: oregon` on both the `bhaktisetu` API service and the `darshya-web` service), apps/api/.env (`DATABASE_URL=postgresql://...@aws-1-ap-northeast-1.pooler.supabase.com:6543/postgres?pgbouncer=true`)
  - evidence: render.yaml pins both services to `region: oregon` (us-west-2). The production DATABASE_URL and DIRECT_URL both resolve to `aws-1-ap-northeast-1.pooler.supabase.com` — Tokyo. Oregon<->Tokyo round-trip is ~90-110 ms. I counted the exact number of SQL statements each endpoint issues by proxying the Prisma connection through a TCP counter that parses the Postgres frontend protocol: GET /api/v1/admin/reports = 25 statements, /api/v1/priest/dashboard = 12, /api/v1/admin/dashboard = 11, /api/v1/admin/metrics/investor = 8, /api/v1/wellness-portal/dashboard = 7, /api/v1/admin/pending-approvals = 7, /api/v1/temples/:slug = 6, /api/v1/bookings/my = 5, /api/v1/admin/bookings = 5. Most of these are sequential awaits, not Promise.all. At ~95 ms per round trip that is ~2.4 s of pure network latency for /admin/reports and ~1.1 s for the pandit dashboard before Postgres does any work. The target market is India, so the user path is India -> Cloudflare -> Oregon -> Tokyo -> Oregon -> India. Locally (API and DB on the same machine) the same endpoints returned in 15-77 ms, which is the same query work with the network removed — that difference is entirely geography.
  - customer_impact: Every authenticated screen in the product feels broken-slow for the exact users you are targeting. A pandit opening his dashboard in Gurugram waits over a second for network alone; the founder's /admin/reports page waits ~2.5 s. It also means you cannot fix latency by optimizing code — the floor is set by the region choice.
  - fix: Colocate the API and the database. Cheapest correct move: create the Supabase project in ap-south-1 (Mumbai) and set both Render services to `region: singapore`, which puts you ~50 ms from Indian users and ~5 ms from the DB. If migrating the Supabase project is too disruptive right now, move Render to `region: singapore` alone — Singapore<->Tokyo is ~70 ms, still better than 95 ms, and Singapore<->India is far better than Oregon<->India. Separately, convert the sequential awaits in the heaviest handlers (admin.service.getReports, priest dashboard) into Promise.all batches so the round trips overlap instead of stacking.

### [SCAL-03] P0/scalability/S No created_at index on any table that grows — every admin list is a full-table scan plus sort
  - where: apps/api/prisma/schema.prisma — models User, PujaBooking, Commission, Payment, Transaction, Review, ContentItem all lack @@index([createdAt]); confirmed against the live database via pg_indexes
  - evidence: pg_indexes for the growth tables shows: users -> btree(id), btree(email), btree(phone), partial btree(blocked) — nothing on created_at, and no index at all on any list/sort column. payments -> btree(gateway_payment_id, user_id, status, id). puja_bookings -> 10 indexes but none on created_at. commissions -> btree(provider_id, status, store_order_id, wellness_enrollment_id, payout_request_id) — none on created_at. transactions -> btree(user_id, type) only. I then built shadow tables with `CREATE TABLE _scal_x (LIKE x INCLUDING ALL)` (so identical columns AND identical indexes), loaded 200,000 synthetic rows each, ANALYZEd, and ran EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS). BEFORE — `SELECT * FROM users ORDER BY created_at DESC LIMIT 20` (this is admin.service.getUsers:107-113): Parallel Seq Scan over 200,000 rows + top-N heapsort, Execution Time 155.353 ms warm / 2,113.550 ms cold. `SELECT * FROM puja_bookings ORDER BY created_at DESC LIMIT 20`: Parallel Seq Scan, 224.884 ms warm / 1,463.063 ms cold. `commissions ORDER BY created_at DESC LIMIT 50` (admin.service.getRevenue:466): 893.192 ms. `transactions WHERE status=? ORDER BY created_at DESC LIMIT 10` (getDashboard:370-374): 1,418.308 ms. AFTER adding `(created_at DESC)` indexes (total build time for all five tables: 3,691 ms): users list 0.109 ms (Index Scan), bookings list 0.156 ms, commissions top-50 0.111 ms, transactions top-10 0.146 ms. That is a 1,400x improvement on the user list and ~10,000x on the transaction list.
  - customer_impact: Today the admin panel is usable because there are 23 users. At 200k users the founder's own dashboard, the user list, the bookings list and the revenue page each take 1-2 seconds of database time *before* the 95 ms Tokyo round trip, and Postgres burns the entire table through shared buffers to answer each one. On Supabase free's shared CPU these scans will also slow down concurrent devotee traffic, so an admin refreshing a list degrades the storefront.
  - fix: Add to schema.prisma and migrate: `@@index([createdAt(sort: Desc)])` on User, PujaBooking, Commission, Transaction, Review, ContentItem; `@@index([status, createdAt(sort: Desc)])` on Payment and Transaction (both are always filtered by status then sorted by date); `@@index([providerId, status, payoutRequestId])` on Commission for the payout-balance query. Follow docs/handoff guidance and apply with plain CREATE INDEX (not CONCURRENTLY) since Prisma wraps each migration in a transaction. Index build measured at 3.7 s for five 200k-row tables, so this is a non-event on today's data — do it before the tables grow, not after.

### [SCAL-04] P1/scalability/M 111 of 205 findMany calls have no take or cursor; the worst ship 15-33 MB per request
  - where: apps/api/src/services/admin.service.ts:368 and :467; apps/api/src/routes/admin-metrics.ts:99, :120, :125, :193, :197, :201, :205; apps/api/src/services/payout.service.ts:159, :165, :179; apps/api/src/routes/store-admin.ts:219 and :341; apps/api/src/services/whatsapp-broadcast.service.ts:196, :229
  - evidence: A parser over every non-test .ts file in apps/api/src found 205 findMany call sites, 111 of which contain neither `take:` nor `cursor:`. I then measured what those actually transfer, using PostgreSQL 18's `EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, SERIALIZE)` against the 200k-row shadow tables — this reports the exact bytes Postgres serializes to the client: /admin/metrics/funnel users query (`user.findMany({where:{createdAt:{gte:since}}, select:{createdAt,phone,email}})`, admin-metrics.ts:197) = output 15,040 kB. Same endpoint's bookings query (admin-metrics.ts:201) = 13,282 kB. /admin/metrics/investor `payment.findMany({where:{status:'captured'}, select:{userId}, distinct:['userId']})` (admin-metrics.ts:99) = 16,016 kB. `prisma.userRoleAssignment.findMany()` in admin.service.ts:368 — no `where` clause at all, the entire table, only to count roles in a JS for-loop — equivalent unbounded read = 33,360 kB. payout.service.getPayoutBalance issues THREE separate `commission.findMany({select:{amount}})` (lines 159/165/179) and sums them in JS with Decimal.add; equivalent read = 10,309 kB. By contrast a properly bounded query (`ORDER BY created_at DESC LIMIT 20`) serializes 9 kB. store-admin.ts:219 loads every order of the current month *including the items JSON* to compute five scalars and a top-10 list; store-admin.ts:341 loads a full YEAR of orders the same way. whatsapp-broadcast.service.ts:196/229 load every booking that was ever confirmed/completed just to derive a phone list.
  - customer_impact: Two costs. First, memory: Render free gives the API container ~512 MB, and a single /admin/metrics/funnel load at 200k users pulls ~28 MB of rows into Node and then buckets them in JavaScript — a few concurrent admin/cron hits and the container OOMs and restarts, taking every devotee's request with it. Second, money: Supabase free allows 5 GB/month egress; at 28 MB per funnel load that is ~180 loads for the entire month, and you have a daily funnel-drop-analyzer cron already hitting it, which alone would consume ~840 MB/month at that scale.
  - fix: Push the aggregation into SQL. admin.service.ts:368 -> `prisma.userRoleAssignment.groupBy({ by: ['role'], _count: true })`. payout.service.ts:159/165/179 -> `prisma.commission.aggregate({ where: {...}, _sum: { amount: true } })`, three aggregates instead of three full reads (the Decimal correctness the comment protects is preserved — Postgres SUM on numeric is exact). admin-metrics.ts:193-205 -> replace the four findMany-then-bucket-in-JS calls with `groupBy({ by: [date_trunc bucket] , _count })` or a single `$queryRaw` with `date_trunc('day', created_at)`. admin-metrics.ts:99/120/125 -> `count({ distinct })` or a raw `SELECT count(DISTINCT user_id)` instead of materializing every userId. store-admin.ts:219/341 -> `aggregate`/`groupBy` for the totals plus a separate `findMany({ take: 10, orderBy: { createdAt: 'desc' } })` for the recent-orders strip. whatsapp-broadcast -> paginate the recipient scan with a cursor. Then add a lint rule or a test that fails on `findMany(` without `take`/`cursor` in routes/ and services/.

### [SCAL-05] P1/scalability/M Every authenticated request in production makes a live network call to Supabase Auth
  - where: apps/api/src/middlewares/auth.ts:243 (`await supabaseAdmin.auth.getUser(token)`), then :257-269 (up to three user.findUnique/findFirst) and :284 (userRoleAssignment.findMany)
  - evidence: authenticateUser has three branches. The `demo-` branch (auth.ts:127-172) costs one `user.findUnique` — that is the branch every number in this report used, because in dev `/api/v1/auth/login` returns a demo token (all eight seeded accounts returned tokens of the form `demo-demo-<role>-token`). Production disables demo tokens (`NODE_ENV !== 'production' || ALLOW_DEMO_TOKENS === 'true'`, auth.ts:114-115), so real users fall through to line 243: `const { data: { user: supabaseUser }, error } = await supabaseAdmin.auth.getUser(token)` — an outbound HTTPS request to Supabase GoTrue in ap-northeast-1 on EVERY request. That is followed by 1-3 Prisma user lookups (id, then email, then phone) plus a `userRoleAssignment.findMany`. There is no local JWT verification and no cache of the verification result anywhere in the file. So the production cost of a request I measured at "1 statement" is really 1 cross-Pacific HTTPS call + 2-4 cross-Pacific SQL statements.
  - customer_impact: Every authenticated page load pays an extra ~100-300 ms third-party round trip that no amount of query tuning removes, and your API's throughput ceiling becomes Supabase Auth's rate limit rather than your own capacity. If Supabase Auth has a slow minute, every logged-in devotee, pandit and temple manager is locked out simultaneously — the middleware returns 500 "Authentication service unavailable" (auth.ts:333) rather than degrading. It also means all authenticated latency numbers in this report are optimistic.
  - fix: Verify the Supabase JWT locally instead of calling out. Supabase signs access tokens with the project JWT secret (or, for asymmetric keys, a JWKS you fetch once and cache); use `jose`/`jsonwebtoken` to verify signature + exp + aud offline — zero network. Keep the remote getUser call only as a fallback for tokens that fail local verification. Then cache the (userId -> {roles, blocked}) lookup in-process with a 60 s TTL so the role findMany does not run per request either. This turns a 4-5 round-trip auth into one cheap local verify plus one cached lookup.

### [SCAL-06] P1/scalability/S aarti-notify re-reads the entire global device-token table inside a per-temple loop
  - where: apps/api/src/jobs/aarti-notify.ts:90 (temples), :105 (`for (const temple of temples)`), :107 (`for (const slot of slots)`), :119 (temple_follows), :127 (tokens by follower), :135 (global aartiOptIn tokens — inside both loops)
  - evidence: The job loads every live-darshan temple with `temple.findMany({ where: { liveDarshanEnabled: true, status: 'active' } })` — unbounded — then iterates temples and, inside that, aarti slots. Per firing slot it issues three unbounded queries: `followModel.findMany({ where: { templeId: temple.id }, select: { userId } })`, `tokenModel.findMany({ where: { userId: { in: followerIds } } })`, and — the expensive one — `tokenModel.findMany({ where: { aartiOptIn: true }, select: { userId, deviceToken, aartiOptIn } })`, which is loop-invariant and re-reads the whole global opt-in list on every iteration. Schema check: FcmDeviceToken has `@@index([userId])` and a unique on deviceToken, but nothing on `aartiOptIn`, so that third query is a sequential scan of the full token table each time. The code's own comment at line 133-134 concedes it: "This is broad — fine for early launch when audience is small." With 100 temples firing a 06:00 aarti in one tick and 100k opted-in devices, that is 100 sequential scans returning 100k rows each — ~10 million rows pulled into a 512 MB Render free container in a single cron tick.
  - customer_impact: The morning-aarti push is the highest-value engagement moment in a devotional app, and it is the exact moment this job will OOM or time out. When it dies mid-loop the temples processed after the crash get no notification at all, silently — and because master-cron is triggered by an external HTTP POST, a timeout looks like a failed webhook, not a product outage.
  - fix: Hoist the global opt-in query out of both loops (read it once per run, before the `for (const temple of temples)` at line 105) and add `@@index([aartiOptIn])` to FcmDeviceToken. Better still, restructure to two queries total per run: one to fetch followers for all firing temple ids in a single `IN (...)`, one for the global opt-ins, then merge in memory. Add `take` + cursor paging to both so the run is bounded regardless of audience size, and cap `temple.findMany` at line 90.

### [SCAL-07] P1/resilience/S Cold start runs 80 sequential DDL statements against Tokyo before serving the first request
  - where: apps/api/src/lib/ensure-schema.ts (80 statements in the STATEMENTS array, applied one at a time via `await prisma.$executeRawUnsafe(sql)` in a for-loop), called from apps/api/src/server.ts:127 before any route registers; render.yaml `startCommand: pnpm --filter @poojly/api start` which is `prisma migrate deploy && node dist/server.js`
  - evidence: ensureSchemaInvariants iterates an 80-statement array of `ALTER TABLE ... ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS` / `CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS` / `CREATE INDEX IF NOT EXISTS` DDL, awaiting each one individually with no batching. At the measured Oregon<->Tokyo round trip of ~95 ms that is ~7.6 seconds of pure network wait on every boot, and it happens *before* `bootstrap()` registers a single route. It runs after `prisma migrate deploy`, which itself connects to Tokyo and reads the _prisma_migrations table. docs/free-tier-budgets.md states Render free "spins down after 15 min idle. Cold-start = 30-60s" — this adds to that, not instead of it. Note also that `ALTER TABLE ... ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS` still acquires an ACCESS EXCLUSIVE lock even when it no-ops, so these 80 statements briefly lock most of your tables on every restart.
  - customer_impact: The first devotee to arrive after any quiet period — which on a pre-launch product is most arrivals — waits 40-90 seconds. Cloudflare's origin timeout is 100 s, so you are one slow boot away from a 524 error page on your own homepage, and the visitor never comes back. During a deploy, the 80 table locks land on top of live traffic.
  - fix: Gate the whole thing: `if (process.env.RUN_SCHEMA_INVARIANTS !== 'true') return;` and turn it on only when you are knowingly recovering from a skipped migration. The start command already runs `prisma migrate deploy`, which is the real mechanism — ensure-schema.ts exists because that step was once bypassed (its own header says so), and that condition no longer holds. If you keep it, send all 80 statements as one multi-statement `$executeRawUnsafe` so it costs one round trip instead of eighty. Separately, add a warm-up pinger or move to a paid Render instance before launch day — every free-tier cold start is a lost first impression.

### [SCAL-08] P1/cost/S The Redis cache the hot catalog paths depend on is not configured — every read hits Postgres
  - where: apps/api/src/lib/cache.ts:10 (`const REDIS_URL = process.env.REDIS_URL || process.env.UPSTASH_REDIS_URL || ''`), apps/api/src/services/temple.service.ts:80 and :126 (cached() wrappers), CACHE_TTL in cache.ts; docs/free-tier-budgets.md (no Redis/Upstash row)
  - evidence: temple.service.findAll wraps its findMany+count in `cached(cacheKey, CACHE_TTL.TEMPLE_LIST /* 300s */, ...)`, findBySlug in a 600 s cache, and panchang in a 6-hour cache. But `getClient()` returns null whenever REDIS_URL and UPSTASH_REDIS_URL are both empty, making cacheGet/cacheSet no-ops. Neither variable appears in apps/api/.env (keys present: DATABASE_URL, DIRECT_URL, SUPABASE_URL, SUPABASE_ANON_KEY, SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY, NODE_ENV, PORT, CORS_ORIGIN, JWT_SECRET, GROQ_API_KEY, COHERE_API_KEY, ENABLE_DEMO_PAY), and nothing is listening on 127.0.0.1:6379 (ECONNREFUSED). I confirmed the effect through the counting proxy: GET /api/v1/temples?limit=20 issues 2 SQL statements on every single call — the SELECT plus the COUNT — with no cache hit ever. Also note docs/free-tier-budgets.md, which is explicitly the gate document for external services, has no line for Redis or Upstash at all, so this dependency has never been budgeted.
  - customer_impact: The three most-hit public endpoints — temple list, temple detail, panchang — are designed to be served from cache and are instead served from a database on another continent, on every request. That is both the latency your homepage visitors feel and the Supabase egress you are paying for with your 5 GB/month allowance. It is also a landmine: the code reads as if caching is on, so future performance work will be measured against a false baseline.
  - fix: Either provision Upstash Redis free tier (10k commands/day, which covers a 5-minute TTL on catalog reads comfortably), set REDIS_URL in Render, and add the row to docs/free-tier-budgets.md — or, if you would rather not add a dependency, add an in-process LRU fallback inside cache.ts so `cached()` degrades to a per-container memory cache instead of a no-op. The second option is free, needs no new service, and would eliminate most of the temple/puja/panchang query load on a single-instance deployment.

### [SCAL-09] P2/scalability/S Admin user list filters by role in JavaScript after pagination, and searches with an unindexed ILIKE
  - where: apps/api/src/services/admin.service.ts:99-105 (search), :107-115 (paginated query), :119-121 (role assignments), :141-143 (`const filtered = filters.role ? enriched.filter(...) : enriched`), :145 (returns unfiltered `total`)
  - evidence: getUsers pages the users table with skip/take, then fetches role assignments for that page, then applies `filters.role` with a JS `.filter()` on the 20 rows it already fetched — while returning `total` from the *unfiltered* `prisma.user.count({ where })`. So filtering by ADMIN returns however many of page 1's 20 users happen to be admins, alongside a total that counts everyone. Search is `{ contains: filters.search, mode: 'insensitive' }` across fullName/email/phone, which Prisma renders as `ILIKE '%x%'`. EXPLAIN (ANALYZE) on the 200k-row shadow users table: `Parallel Seq Scan, Filter: ((full_name ~~* '%ram%') OR (email ~~* '%ram%')), Rows Removed by Filter: 100000` per worker, Execution Time 711.818 ms. There is no pg_trgm index on the table (pg_indexes shows only id/email/phone/blocked).
  - customer_impact: Support cannot find a user. Searching a name takes ~0.7 s of database time at 200k users and gets slower linearly; filtering by role shows a nearly-empty page with a wrong count, so the operator concludes there are no pandits when there are thousands. Both are the tools you will reach for first when a real devotee reports a problem.
  - fix: Move the role filter into the query: `where.roleAssignments = { some: { role: filters.role } }` on the User model so both the page and the count reflect it. For search, add `CREATE EXTENSION IF NOT EXISTS pg_trgm;` plus GIN trigram indexes on users(full_name) and users(email), which makes ILIKE '%x%' index-assisted; or switch to a prefix search (`startsWith`) which a plain btree can serve. Cap `limit` the way the public routes do (`Math.min(Number(limit) || 20, 100)`) — right now `filters.limit` is passed through unbounded from apps/api/src/routes/admin.ts:188.

### [SCAL-10] P2/cost/M Two Render free web services, but the free tier only funds one always-on — and the budget doc says so
  - where: render.yaml — service `bhaktisetu` (API, `plan: free`, `region: oregon`) and service `darshya-web` (docker, `plan: free`, `region: oregon`); docs/free-tier-budgets.md Render rules
  - evidence: render.yaml declares two `plan: free` web services. docs/free-tier-budgets.md lists the Render web-service cap as "750 hr/mo (always-on free)" and its own rule #2 reads: "One always-on free service possible — currently API. Don't add a second free service." Two services running continuously is 2 x ~730 = ~1,460 instance-hours/month against a 750-hour allowance. The practical consequence is that both services must sleep to stay inside the cap, and both carry `healthCheckPath` (/health and /) that only fires while they are awake. Compounding: the web container server-renders pages by calling the API (measured: 0 browser-originated /api/v1 requests on /, /temples, /pujas, /dashboard), so a cold web request can trigger a cold API start — two spin-ups chained, plus the 80-statement DDL replay from SCAL-07.
  - customer_impact: You cannot keep the site warm within the free tier. Whichever service was idle when a visitor arrives makes them wait, and if both were idle they wait twice. That is the difference between a marketing push converting and bouncing.
  - fix: Decide explicitly which service gets the 750 hours. Cheapest real fix: move the web front end to a platform whose free tier does not sleep (Vercel Hobby or Cloudflare Pages for the static/ISR portions) and keep the single Render free service for the API — that also restores the doc's stated invariant. Otherwise, budget for one paid Render Starter instance for the API before launch and document the change in docs/free-tier-budgets.md rather than letting the two services quietly contend.

### [SCAL-11] P2/cost/S Resend's 100 emails/day is the first hard business cap you will hit — around 30 signups a day
  - where: docs/free-tier-budgets.md (Resend row: "100/day, 3000/mo" and Resend rule #1: "Welcome + receipt only. No marketing blast. 100/day cap = ~3 per signup")
  - evidence: The budget doc itself states the arithmetic: 100 emails/day at ~3 per signup is ~33 signups/day, and that figure has to cover welcome mails, booking receipts, 80G donation receipts, password resets and referral notifications from the same allowance. The doc also records that six onboarding drip agents were paused in agents-daily.yml precisely because RESEND_API_KEY is unset — so the drip layer is currently dark, and turning it on consumes the same 100/day. TASKS.md separately records an unresolved Supabase built-in mailer limit of "2 emails/hour project-wide" for auth mails until SMTP is pointed at Resend, which is a tighter cap still on password resets and confirmations.
  - customer_impact: On the day a temple partnership or a press mention lands, devotee number 34 signs up and never receives a welcome or a booking receipt — for a puja service where the receipt IS the proof of purchase, that reads as fraud. Failures are silent: Resend returns 429 and the send path is env-or-noop.
  - fix: Instrument first — log every send with a daily counter and alert at 70/day so you find out before customers do. Then split the traffic: transactional receipts and password resets stay on Resend, and move the digest/drip/marketing sends to a separate provider or to WhatsApp (which you are already building for). Point Supabase Auth SMTP at Resend to clear the 2/hour auth-mail ceiling that TASKS.md flags as still open. Budget the paid Resend tier ($20/mo for 50k) as a launch-day line item — this is the cheapest constraint on the list to buy your way out of.

### [SCAL-12] P3/cost/S Supabase's 500 MB storage cap is NOT your problem — measured ~1.1 KB per completed order
  - where: measured against the live schema via shadow tables built with `CREATE TABLE _scal_x (LIKE x INCLUDING ALL)` at 200,000 rows each
  - evidence: Actual on-disk sizes including all indexes at 200k rows: _scal_puja_bookings 86 MB (~440 B/row, of which 50 MB is its 10 indexes), _scal_commissions 50 MB (~250 B/row), _scal_payments 41 MB (~205 B/row), _scal_transactions 37 MB (~185 B/row), _scal_users 30 MB (~150 B/row). One completed order writes roughly one row into each of bookings/payments/commissions/transactions = ~1.08 KB fully indexed. Against Supabase free's 500 MB, and reserving half the budget for users, content, chat and logs, that is on the order of 230,000 completed orders before the cap binds. The live seeded database today is 3.88 MB total across 86 tables with 232 rows.
  - customer_impact: None yet, and none for a long time — at ₹3,500 per puja that headroom is roughly ₹80 crore of lifetime GMV. This is here so you do not spend engineering time on it: storage is the last constraint to break, well behind the rate limiter, Resend, cold starts and egress.
  - fix: No action needed on storage. Do watch egress instead (5 GB/month), which the unbounded reads in SCAL-04 burn far faster than rows accumulate. Follow the existing doc rule to prune any *_logs/*_metrics tables and never store full request bodies, and re-measure once outreach_logs and chat_messages have real volume — those are the two tables whose per-row size is unbounded by design.

### [SCAL-13] P3/scalability/S Healthy: no per-row N+1, real pagination, and concurrency-safe booking writes
  - where: verified across apps/api/src/routes/* and apps/api/src/services/* by counting SQL statements on the wire
  - evidence: I put a TCP proxy in front of Postgres that parses the frontend protocol and counts every Parse/Query/Execute message, then ran a second API instance through it and measured one request at a time (idle control: 0 statements). Statement counts are CONSTANT with respect to row count, not linear: /api/v1/temples?limit=20 = 2 (findMany + COUNT), /api/v1/pujas/types = 2, /api/v1/priests = 2, /api/v1/content = 2, /api/v1/bookings/my = 5, /api/v1/users/me/bookings = 5, /api/v1/priest/bookings = 5, /api/v1/admin/bookings = 5. Related entities are hydrated with a single `WHERE id IN (?,?,...)` batch per relation — literal example from /admin/bookings: `SELECT ... FROM "public"."temples" WHERE "public"."temples"."id" IN (...)`. Pagination is genuine skip/take with a separate COUNT and a hard cap (`Math.min(Number(limit) || 20, 100)` in routes/temples.ts:29 and routes/content.ts:23). Write concurrency: 20 parallel `POST /api/v1/bookings/book` for the identical temple/offering/date/slot returned {"201":5,"409":15} in 1,577 ms — five accepted up to the slot capacity, fifteen cleanly rejected with `{"code":"SLOT_UNAVAILABLE"}`, zero 500s, zero deadlocks, zero timeouts. 50 parallel reads on /api/v1/temples returned 50/50 HTTP 200 in 1,247 ms. /health stayed 200 throughout every throttle test.
  - customer_impact: Positive. The data-access layer will not fall over from query fan-out as the catalog grows, and double-booking under a Navratri thundering herd is genuinely prevented at the database layer rather than hoped away. Fixing the region, the rate limiter and the indexes gets you a fast product — you do not also have to rewrite the query layer.
  - fix: No change required. Protect it: add a regression test asserting that /api/v1/temples issues exactly 2 statements and /api/v1/bookings/my exactly 5 regardless of seeded row count (the project already has an N+1 detector at apps/api/src/db/n-plus-one-detector.ts that warns above 5 identical templates per request scope — wire `reportNPlusOne` into CI rather than only into runtime logs). Keep the concurrent-slot test as a permanent integration test.

## AREA: accessibility   score=42

### [A11Y-01] P0/a11y/S --color-surface-500 (the default muted-text token) fails 4.5:1 on every background it is used on
  - where: apps/web/app/globals.css:17 (--color-surface-500: #7a756e, commented --color-text-muted); 1,236 uses of text-surface-500 across 236 .tsx files under apps/web
  - evidence: Computed contrast against the four palette backgrounds: #7a756e on #f5f3ef (surface-50, the body background) = 4.12:1; on #faf9f6 (surface-100, cards) = 4.34:1; on #ede9e3 (surface-200, section bands) = 3.78:1; on #2a2520 (the footer) = 3.32:1. All below the 4.5:1 required for normal-size text. Pixel-verified against the real rendered raster on live pages: '/pujas' hero subhead 18px/400 = 3.78; '/store' hero subhead 18px/400 = 3.78; '/admin' "Platform overview and key metrics" 13.44px/400 = 4.12; '/auth/login' "Continue your spiritual journey" = 3.78 and "Don't have an account?" = 3.70; footer body copy "Bringing the divine into daily life…" = 3.32 on all 7 pages. Usage counted with ripgrep: 1,236 occurrences / 236 files.
  - customer_impact: This is the token used for every secondary line in the product — pricing subtext, temple/pandit metadata, form helper text, dashboard captions, the footer. Users with mild low vision or presbyopia (a large share of a devotional audience skewing older) and anyone reading on a phone in Indian daylight lose the supporting text that explains what each booking includes. Because it is the default, the failure is on every screen simultaneously rather than isolated to one page.
  - fix: Darken the token in apps/web/app/globals.css:17. #6b6660 gives 4.90:1 on #f5f3ef and 5.16:1 on #faf9f6; #656059 gives 5.2:1+ on all three light surfaces. Note the token must also work on the dark footer (#2a2520) — either bump surface-500 to a value that fails there too and give the footer its own lighter muted token (e.g. #b5b0a8 reaches 7.04:1 on #2a2520), or add --color-text-muted-on-dark. One CSS edit plus one footer override; no component changes needed since everything consumes the token.

### [A11Y-02] P0/a11y/S --color-surface-400 is the global ::placeholder colour at 2.05:1 — every form field's placeholder is effectively unreadable
  - where: apps/web/app/globals.css:16 (--color-surface-400: #b5b0a8, commented --color-text-faint) and globals.css:265-267 (::placeholder { color: var(--color-text-faint); opacity: 1; }); 613 uses of text-surface-400 across 137 files
  - evidence: Read directly off the ::placeholder pseudo-element via getComputedStyle(el, '::placeholder') on /auth/login: the email field placeholder "you@example.com" and password placeholder "Enter password" both compute #b5b0a8 on #faf9f6 = 2.05:1 at 16px/400 (needs 4.5:1). Token maths across backgrounds: 1.78:1 on #ede9e3, 1.95:1 on #f5f3ef, 2.05:1 on #faf9f6, 2.16:1 on #ffffff.
