Pixel-verified instances of the same token as visible text: /store strike-through compare-at price "₹1,899" = 2.05, /store review count "342" = 2.04, /pujas "onwards" = 2.05, /admin "Last updated: 01:54 pm" = 1.95, booking-wizard inactive step label "Devotee Details" = 1.95.
  - customer_impact: Placeholders are the only hint on several unlabelled fields (see A11Y-05, A11Y-06, A11Y-07), so a user who cannot resolve 2:1 text has no idea what to type into the newsletter box, the store search, or the booking date field. On /store the strike-through original price is the discount proof — invisible at 2.05:1, the 'up to 50% off' claim reads as unsubstantiated. On the booking wizard the inactive step names disappear, so the user cannot see how many steps remain.
  - fix: Two changes in apps/web/app/globals.css. (1) Stop using text-faint for placeholders: set ::placeholder { color: var(--color-text-muted); } once --color-text-muted is fixed per A11Y-01 — placeholder text is text and must meet 4.5:1. (2) Darken --color-surface-400 itself to about #7d7871 (4.5:1 on #faf9f6) or reserve it strictly for non-text decoration (dividers, disabled icon fills) and sweep the 613 text-surface-400 usages onto surface-500. The placeholder line alone is a one-line fix that clears every form field in the app.

### [A11Y-03] P0/a11y/M Four of the five desktop mega-menu panels are unreachable by keyboard (WCAG 2.1.1, Level A)
  - where: apps/web/components/CategoryNav.tsx — onFocus opens the panel (line ~160), onTriggerKeyDown lines 111-130 handles ArrowDown/Enter/Space by calling setOpenKey() only; the shared panel renders after all five triggers
  - evidence: Measured with real Tab keypresses on http://127.0.0.1:3000/. Focusing the first trigger ("Worship") opens the Worship panel. Then: Tab+1 -> "Library" (openMenu now "LIBRARY"), Tab+2 -> "Astrology", Tab+3 -> "Yatra", Tab+4 -> "Shop", Tab+5..10 -> "Storefront", "Prasad", "Idols", "Books", "Rudraksha (18)", "Navaratna (9)" — i.e. the items of the LAST trigger only. Because each trigger re-opens its own panel on focus, and the single shared panel sits after all five triggers in the DOM, the Worship/Library/Astrology/Yatra item lists can never be entered by forward Tab. ArrowDown on the trigger was measured as {"active":"Worship","tag":"BUTTON","movedIntoMenu":false} — the APG menubar contract is declared (aria-haspopup="menu", role="menu", 8 role="menuitem" children measured) but focus is never moved into the menu. Escape does work correctly (openMenus 1 -> 0, focus stays on the trigger). lib/category-tree.ts contains 90 href entries across worship/library/astrology/yatra/shop.
  - customer_impact: Roughly 70 of the 90 primary navigation destinations — the entire Worship, Library, Astrology and Yatra taxonomies — are closed to keyboard-only and switch users on desktop. Those users can still reach content via the footer link farm and search, but the product's main information architecture is inaccessible to them, which is a Level A failure and the kind of thing that draws a legal complaint rather than a bug report.
  - fix: In apps/web/components/CategoryNav.tsx make ArrowDown/Enter/Space move focus into the open panel: after setOpenKey(key), focus the first [role="menuitem"] inside the panel (a ref plus a useEffect that focuses on open). Add ArrowUp/ArrowDown roving movement between menuitems and Escape returning focus to the trigger (Escape already returns focus, so only the entry path is missing). Optionally stop opening on plain onFocus and open only on click/ArrowDown/Enter — that also removes the surprise of panels flying open as a keyboard user tabs across the nav.

### [A11Y-04] P0/a11y/M Mobile nav overlay is not a modal: 176 background controls stay focusable behind it and focus is never moved in or restored
  - where: apps/web/components/MobileNavV2.tsx:106-112 (the <aside>, portalled to document.body)
  - evidence: Measured at 390x844 on http://127.0.0.1:3000/. With the menu OPEN: role=null, aria-modal=null (only aria-label="Mobile navigation"); document.querySelector('main') has neither aria-hidden nor the inert attribute; outsideFocusables=176, insideFocusables=22; activeElement after opening was the trigger itself ("BUTTON:Close menu"), activeInsideAside=false — focus is never moved into the panel. Fifteen consecutive Tab presses were recorded and 15/15 landed OUTSIDE the overlay, on background links the user cannot see ("Explore Temples", "Book a Puja", "Live Darshan", "Ask a Pandit", "Book parvati puja", …). Escape does close the sheet (onScreen true -> false, body overflow hidden -> visible) but focus was left on a background link ("A:Book Puja") rather than restored to the hamburger. Body scroll-lock does work correctly (bodyOverflow visible -> hidden -> visible).
  - customer_impact: On mobile — the majority surface for this product — a keyboard, switch or screen-reader user who opens the menu is immediately lost: the focus ring is drawn on elements hidden behind an opaque full-screen sheet, so there is no visible indication of where they are, and no number of Tab presses reaches the menu links. A screen-reader user is read the whole page behind the sheet as if the menu were not there. Practically, mobile navigation is unusable by assistive technology.
  - fix: In apps/web/components/MobileNavV2.tsx add role="dialog" aria-modal="true" to the <aside>; on open, focus the Close button (or the panel container with tabIndex={-1}); apply inert to the page wrapper while open (or aria-hidden plus a focus-trap loop on Tab/Shift+Tab across the panel's focusable set); on close, return focus to the hamburger trigger. Escape and scroll-lock are already implemented (lines 61-75) so only the role, focus move, background inert and focus restore are missing.

### [A11Y-05] P0/a11y/S Footer copyright line renders at 1.60:1 on every page
  - where: the site footer, present on all 7 pages tested — text-surface-600 (#4a4540) on the footer background #2a2520
  - evidence: Measured on /pujas/rudrabhishek by reading the element's own computed colour and compositing the ancestor background stack: "© 2026 Darshya. All rights res…" = #4a4540 on #2a2520, 13.44px/400, ratio 1.60:1 (needs 4.5:1). The same pair appears in the DOM sweep on /, /pujas, /pujas/book and /auth/login with 28 text runs sharing it. The sibling footer body copy is 3.32:1 (see A11Y-01).
  - customer_impact: The copyright, entity name and legal year are the footer's trust and compliance signals and are effectively invisible to everyone, not only low-vision users — 1.6:1 is barely distinguishable from the background on a good monitor and disappears entirely on a phone outdoors. For an India product carrying 80G receipts and DPDP claims, an unreadable legal footer is a bad look with regulators as well as users.
  - fix: Give the footer its own text tokens rather than reusing the light-surface scale. On #2a2520, #b5b0a8 reaches 7.04:1 and #7a756e reaches 3.32:1 — so map footer body text to surface-400 and footer muted/legal text to surface-300 (#e4dfd8) or #9a948c. A single scoped override on the footer element covers every page.

### [A11Y-06] P1/a11y/S Login email and password inputs have no autocomplete and no name attribute (WCAG 2.1 AA 1.3.5)
  - where: apps/web/app/auth/login/page.tsx:504-514 (email input, id="email") and 538-548 (password input, id="password")
  - evidence: Read off the live DOM on /auth/login: {"sel":"input#email","type":"email","name":"Email Address","from":"label[for]","autocomplete":null} and {"sel":"input#password","type":"password","autocomplete":null}. Source confirms neither element carries an autoComplete or a name attribute. By contrast apps/web/components/EmailCapture.tsx:504 does set autoComplete="email", so the convention exists elsewhere in the codebase. Visible <label for> is correctly wired on both, so the accessible name is fine — only the input-purpose metadata is missing.
  - customer_impact: Password managers and browser autofill cannot reliably identify these fields, so returning users have to type their credentials by hand on a phone keyboard every time. That is a direct conversion tax on the login step, and for users with motor or cognitive impairments — the population SC 1.3.5 exists for — it is the difference between signing in and abandoning. It is also plausibly a contributor to the reported sign-in friction already tracked in TASKS.md.
  - fix: Add autoComplete="email" name="email" to the email input and autoComplete="current-password" name="password" to the password input in apps/web/app/auth/login/page.tsx. Do the same sweep on /auth/signup (autoComplete="new-password") and /auth/forgot-password.

### [A11Y-07] P1/a11y/S Password-visibility toggle on the login form has no accessible name
  - where: apps/web/app/auth/login/page.tsx:550-556
  - evidence: The only unnamed control found across all 7 pages. Live DOM: {"issue":"no-accessible-name","tag":"button","role":null,"html":"<button type=\"button\" class=\"text-surface-400 hover:text-surface-600 absolute top-1/2 right-3 -translate-y-1/2\"><svg …"}. Source shows the button's only child is {showPassword ? <EyeOff/> : <Eye/>} — a lucide SVG with no title, no aria-label on the button, no aria-pressed. Confirmed reachable by keyboard: it is tab stop [2] in the login card, announced by a screen reader as just "button".
  - customer_impact: A screen-reader user tabbing through the login form hits an unlabelled button between the password field and Sign In and has no way to know what it does — the natural guess is that it submits or clears. Users who rely on the reveal toggle because they mistype long passwords (dyslexia, motor tremor, small touch targets) lose it entirely.
  - fix: Add aria-label={showPassword ? 'Hide password' : 'Show password'} and aria-pressed={showPassword} to the button at apps/web/app/auth/login/page.tsx:550, and aria-hidden="true" to the icon. One-line change.

### [A11Y-08] P1/a11y/M Booking wizard is silent to assistive technology: no live region, no step semantics, no field grouping
  - where: apps/web/app/pujas/book/page.tsx — STEP_LABELS at line 144, step sections from line ~1479 onward
  - evidence: Audited the real wizard by loading http://localhost:3000/pujas/book?slug=rudrabhishek as a logged-in devotee (127.0.0.1 cannot be used — see A11Y-16). Measured at the initial step and after each of four Continue attempts, identical every time: {"fieldsets":0,"legends":0,"radiogroups":0,"radios":0,"forms":1,"progressbars":0} and liveRegions: [] — zero elements with aria-live, role="alert" or role="status" anywhere on the page. A grep for aria-current across the file returns nothing, so the four-step indicator built from STEP_LABELS = ['Temple & Date','Devotee Details','Review & Prasad','Payment'] carries no current-step semantics. Temple, tier and slot pickers are clickable divs/buttons, not grouped inputs, so there is no radiogroup and no group label. The inactive step labels also fail contrast at 1.95:1 (#b5b0a8 on #f5f3ef) and the active one at 4.48:1 (#9e6229 on #f5f3ef).
  - customer_impact: This is the revenue path. A screen-reader user who presses Continue gets no announcement that the step changed, no statement of which step they are on or how many remain, and no grouping telling them that eight temple cards are one choice rather than eight separate links. Combined with the invisible inactive step labels, even a sighted low-vision user cannot tell how long the flow is. Booking abandonment here costs an actual transaction, not just a pageview.
  - fix: In apps/web/app/pujas/book/page.tsx: (1) render the step indicator as an <ol> with aria-current="step" on the active item, or add role="progressbar" with aria-valuenow/valuemin/valuemax/aria-valuetext; (2) add a polite live region that announces 'Step 2 of 4, Devotee Details' on each step change, and move focus to the new step's heading (tabIndex={-1}); (3) wrap each choice set (temple, tier, time slot) in a <fieldset> with a <legend>, or role="radiogroup" with aria-labelledby and role="radio" aria-checked on the cards.

### [A11Y-09] P1/a11y/S Booking wizard date field has no label — placeholder "DD/MM/YYYY" only
  - where: apps/web/components/ui/DateInput.tsx:193-203 (the text input) and apps/web/app/pujas/book/page.tsx:1479-1486 (renders <DateInput> without the component's optional label prop)
  - evidence: Live DOM on the working booking wizard: {"tag":"input","type":"text","id":"","name":"","placeholder":"DD/MM/YYYY","ariaLabel":null,"labelFor":false,"wrapped":false,"autocomplete":"off","visible":true}. The component does support a label prop (DateInput.tsx:11, rendered at :189-191) but the booking page's call site at page.tsx:1479 passes only value/min/max/onChange/dateMarkers/disabledDates. The adjacent calendar-open button is correctly labelled (aria-label="Open date picker", DateInput.tsx:209), which makes the bare input the odd one out. The placeholder itself renders at 2.05:1 (A11Y-02), so it is not a usable fallback for sighted users either.
  - customer_impact: The date is the single most consequential field in the booking — it determines the muhurat and whether the puja can be performed at all. A screen-reader user hears 'edit text, blank' with no indication of what date is wanted or in which format. Placeholder-as-label also disappears the moment the user starts typing, so anyone with short-term memory difficulty loses the DD/MM/YYYY format hint exactly when they need it.
  - fix: Pass label="Puja date" from apps/web/app/pujas/book/page.tsx:1479, and in DateInput.tsx wire the label to the input with a useId-generated id/htmlFor pair rather than a floating <label>. Add aria-describedby pointing at a persistent 'Format: DD/MM/YYYY' hint so the format survives typing, and set required/aria-required to match the wizard's validation.

### [A11Y-10] P1/a11y/M Primary CTA fill bg-saffron-500 gives 3.79:1 white text; four accent badge fills are worse (2.29–3.53:1)
  - where: apps/web/app/globals.css:30 (--color-saffron-500: #b87333, aliased --color-primary); bg-saffron-500 used 70 times / 44 files. Accent fills on /pujas and /store product and puja cards
  - evidence: Verified by reading each element's own painted fill on the live pages. /pujas "Book Now" button: #ffffff on #b87333, 13.44px/500 = 3.79:1 (needs 4.5). Cookie-banner "Accept all" (present on every page): #ffffff on #b87333 = 3.79:1. Badge fills measured on /pujas and /store: #ffffff on #0ea5e9 (ganga-blue) = 2.77:1 — also below the 3:1 non-text floor; #ffffff on #d4a438 = 2.29:1; #ffffff on #ec4899 (lotus-pink) = 3.53:1; #ffffff on #16a34a (tulsi-green) = 3.30:1. For contrast, bg-saffron-600 (#9e6229) with white reaches 4.96:1 and passes — /auth/login's own Sign In submit uses it and measured 4.96:1.
  - customer_impact: The buttons that take money — Book Now on the puja catalogue and Accept all on the consent banner — are the ones failing. Label text on a saturated fill at 3.8:1 washes out in sunlight and for anyone with reduced contrast sensitivity, so the primary action is the hardest thing on the card to read. The 'Featured' badges at 2.29–2.77:1 are decoration that carries merchandising meaning nobody can read.
  - fix: Standardise filled buttons on bg-saffron-600 (#9e6229, 4.96:1) — the app already does this on the login submit, so it is a find-and-replace of bg-saffron-500 on any element containing text-white, and saffron-500 stays available for borders and icons. For the badges, either darken the accent fills (a 600-weight of each: #0284c7 gives 4.0, #15803d gives 5.0, #be185d gives 6.0) or invert them to dark text on the light tint, which is the pattern already used successfully elsewhere.

### [A11Y-11] P1/a11y/M Every /store product card nests two buttons inside an anchor — 49 invalid nested-interactive controls and 48 dead tab stops
  - where: apps/web/app/store/page.tsx:265-318 — <a href="/store/[handle]"> wrapping <button aria-label="Add to wishlist"> (line 270) and <button>Add to Cart</button> (line 314)
  - evidence: 49 nested-interactive instances detected on /store (the only page with more than one; / has 1, every other page 0). Sample: {"inner":"button \"\"","outer":"a[/store/premium-brass-puja-thali-set]"} and {"inner":"button \"Add to Cart\"","outer":"a[/store/premium-brass-puja-thali-set]"}. Source confirms 24 product cards each containing an <a> with two <button> descendants. Neither button has an onClick handler — the wishlist button at line 270 and the Add to Cart button at line 314 carry only className and (for wishlist) aria-label, so activating either simply follows the enclosing link to the PDP.
  - customer_impact: Interactive content inside <a> is invalid HTML and assistive technology resolves it inconsistently — some screen readers announce the link with the buttons' text concatenated into its name, so the card reads as 'Premium Brass Puja Thali Set … Add to wishlist … Add to Cart, link'. A keyboard user pays 48 extra tab stops to cross the product grid, and every one of those stops is a control that looks actionable and does nothing but navigate. Users reasonably believe they added an item to the cart when they did not.
  - fix: Restructure the card so the <a> covers only the image and title (or use a stretched-link pseudo-element on the card container) and move both buttons out as siblings of the link, not descendants. While there, give the Add to Cart button a real onClick that adds to the cart, or remove it — a focusable control with no behaviour is worse than no control.

### [A11Y-12] P1/a11y/S Search overlay is not exposed as a dialog and drops focus to <body> on Escape
  - where: the header Search control (button[aria-label="Search"]) and the overlay it opens, on every page
  - evidence: Measured on http://127.0.0.1:3000/ at desktop width. On open: {"dialogs":0,"info":[],"active":"INPUT/Search temples, pujas, aartis…","bodyOverflow":"visible"} — focus is correctly moved into the search input (good), but there is no role="dialog", no aria-modal, and no scroll lock. On Escape: {"dialogs":0,"active":"BODY "} — the overlay closes but focus lands on document.body instead of returning to the Search trigger.
  - customer_impact: Dropping focus to body means the next Tab restarts from the top of the document. A keyboard user who opens search from the header, changes their mind, and presses Escape is thrown back to the skip link and has to tab all the way back to where they were — on a page with 176 focusable controls that is a long walk. Screen-reader users also get no announcement that a dialog opened or closed.
  - fix: Add role="dialog" aria-modal="true" and an accessible name (aria-label="Search Darshya") to the overlay container, lock body scroll while open, and store the trigger element on open so Escape and outside-click can call trigger.focus() on close. The desktop mega-menu in components/CategoryNav.tsx already does the focus-restore correctly and can be copied.

### [A11Y-13] P2/a11y/S Mobile nav sheet keeps 22 focusable elements inside an aria-hidden container when closed
  - where: apps/web/components/MobileNavV2.tsx:106-112 — aria-hidden={!open} with a translate-based hide and pointer-events-none, but no inert and no visibility:hidden
  - evidence: Measured at 390x844 with the menu closed: {"found":true,"ariaHidden":"true","display":"flex","visibility":"visible","inert":false,"focusableCount":22,"stillLaidOut":22,"sample":["Darshya","Close menu","","Worship\nWatch the divine"]}. This is the classic axe aria-hidden-focus violation: focusable descendants of an aria-hidden subtree. Honest scope note — I tabbed 30 times from page top and never entered the aside (the sheet is portalled to the end of document.body, so it comes last in tab order); the defect is confirmed statically but a user must exhaust the whole page first to hit it.
  - customer_impact: A keyboard user who tabs to the end of a short mobile page, or uses Shift+Tab from the address bar to reach the end of the document, lands on 22 invisible off-screen controls with a visible focus ring drawn nowhere on screen. Screen readers are told the subtree does not exist while the browser still hands focus to it, which is the exact contradiction assistive tech handles worst.
  - fix: Add the inert attribute to the <aside> when !open (React 19 / Next 15 supports inert as a boolean prop), which removes both focusability and AT exposure in one attribute and makes the aria-hidden redundant. Alternatively toggle visibility:hidden alongside the translate, which also removes descendants from the tab order.

### [A11Y-14] P2/a11y/S Four dangling aria-controls references on every page of the site
  - where: apps/web/components/MobileNavV2.tsx:169 (aria-controls={panelId} set unconditionally) versus line 187 (<ul id={panelId}> rendered only when isOpenAcc)
  - evidence: Detected on all 7 pages, always the same four: mobile-acc-library, mobile-acc-astrology, mobile-acc-yatra, mobile-acc-shop. The fifth accordion (worship) is open by default so its panel exists and its reference resolves. Source confirms the id only exists inside the {isOpenAcc && (<ul id={panelId}>…)} branch. The desktop equivalent gets this right — apps/web/components/CategoryNav.tsx:156 uses aria-controls={isOpen ? panelId : undefined}.
  - customer_impact: Low severity but a genuine axe aria-valid-attr-value failure that will show up in any automated scan a partner, investor or enterprise customer runs against the site. Some screen readers report the broken relationship as an error to the user.
  - fix: Mirror the desktop pattern: change apps/web/components/MobileNavV2.tsx:169 to aria-controls={isOpenAcc ? panelId : undefined}. One-line change that clears the finding on all 7 pages at once.

### [A11Y-15] P2/a11y/S Structural nits: /auth/login has no h1, /store skips h1 to h3, /admin nests <main> inside <main>
  - where: apps/web/app/auth/login/page.tsx (page heading is an h2); apps/web/app/store/page.tsx (sale banner h3 and 24 product-title h3s directly under the h1); apps/web/app/layout.tsx:399 (<main id="main">) plus apps/web/app/admin/layout.tsx:177 (a second <main>)
  - evidence: Measured heading sequences: /auth/login = h2,h3,h3,h3,h3,h3,h4,h4,h4,h4,h2 with h1Count=0. /store = h1,h3 x25,h2,… — one skipped-level violation recorded, from h1 "Bhakti Store" straight to h3 "Akshaya Tritiya Sale — Up to 50% Off!". /admin landmark counts: {"main":2,"nav":4,"header":2,"footer":1} versus {"main":1,"nav":3,"header":1,"footer":1} on every other page. All other pages had exactly one h1 and zero skipped levels.
  - customer_impact: Screen-reader users navigate by heading and by landmark. A missing h1 on the login page means the H-key jump list has no top-level entry, so the user cannot orient to 'this is the sign-in page'. Two <main> landmarks on /admin make the landmark rotor ambiguous — 'main, main' with no way to tell which holds the dashboard. The h1-to-h3 jump on /store implies a missing product-grid section that does not exist.
  - fix: Promote the login card's page heading from h2 to h1 in apps/web/app/auth/login/page.tsx. On /store, either demote the product titles to h3 under a real h2 section heading ('All products') or promote them to h2. In apps/web/app/admin/layout.tsx:177 change the inner <main> to a <div> (or <section aria-label="Admin content">) since the root layout already provides the main landmark.

### [A11Y-16] P2/a11y/S Local API CORS allowlist omits 127.0.0.1, which hides every client-rendered form behind a catalog error
  - where: the API CORS configuration in apps/api versus apps/web/.env NEXT_PUBLIC_API_URL=http://localhost:4000/api/v1
  - evidence: Reproduced with curl: a request to http://localhost:4000/api/v1/pujas/types/rudrabhishek with Origin: http://localhost:3000 returns 200 with access-control-allow-origin: http://localhost:3000, while the identical request with Origin: http://127.0.0.1:3000 returns 200 with no access-control-allow-origin header at all — so the browser blocks it. Consequence on /pujas/book?slug=rudrabhishek loaded from 127.0.0.1:3000: the wizard rendered its error state ("We couldn't reach the catalog. Please check your c…"), h1Count=0, only 22 text runs on the page and zero form fields. Re-running the same audit against http://localhost:3000 produced the real four-step wizard with h1Count=1 and the date field, which is where findings A11Y-08 and A11Y-09 come from.
  - customer_impact: No end-user impact in production — this is a local/dev-harness issue. Flagging it because it silently voids any QA, screenshot or accessibility run performed against 127.0.0.1: the pages return HTTP 200 and look plausible, so a scan reports a thin, error-state page as if it were the product. Two of my own passes were wasted on it before I caught the discrepancy.
  - fix: Add http://127.0.0.1:3000 (and http://127.0.0.1:4000 if needed) to the API's development CORS allowlist alongside http://localhost:3000, or standardise all local tooling and docs on the localhost origin. Worth a line in the test README either way.

## AREA: i18n   score=18

### [I-01] P0/i18n/L The web translation function has no locale parameter — it can only ever return English
  - where: apps/web/lib/t.ts (whole file); apps/web/lib/i18n/
  - evidence: t.ts line 1 is `import en from './i18n/en.json'`. build() does `const dict = en[ns] as Record<string,string>` — `en` is a module-level constant, not a parameter. All three exports take only a namespace: `getTranslations<N>(ns: N)`, `useTranslations<N>(ns: N)`, `t<N>(ns: N)`. No signature accepts a locale, and no caller could pass one. `ls apps/web/lib/i18n/` returns exactly one entry: en.json (15,362 bytes, 14 namespaces, 374 keys — nav 32, common 34, home 80, temples 19, pujas 21, darshan 12, donations 20, content 18, store 17, auth 33, upgrade 27, yatra 13, terms 17, footer 31). 8 page files import it: app/page.tsx, app/pujas/page.tsx, app/temples (via api), app/darshan/page.tsx, app/donations/page.tsx, app/store/page.tsx, app/auth/login/page.tsx, app/upgrade/page.tsx, app/yatra/destinations/page.tsx. There is no code path by which any of the 374 keys could resolve to a non-English string.
  - customer_impact: Every string routed through the app's own translation layer is English for every user in every language, permanently. This is not a gap in coverage that more translation work would close — the plumbing to deliver a translated string does not exist, so commissioning hi/ta/te/bn/mr copy today would produce files nothing can read. 22 of the 23 seeded users have preferred_language='hi'.
  - fix: Give the translator a locale: add `lib/i18n/{hi,ta,te,bn,mr}.json` keyed identically to en.json, change build() to `build<N>(ns: N, locale: Locale)` selecting from a `CATALOGS: Record<Locale, Messages>` map with per-key English fallback, and resolve the active locale from a request-scoped source (cookie or path segment) rather than module scope. Server components read it via `cookies()`; client components via a LocaleProvider context. Keep the existing key names so the 8 call sites need no edit beyond threading the locale.

### [I-02] P0/i18n/M The only language switcher on 8 of 8 product pages leads to marketing shells that link straight back to English
  - where: apps/web/app/layout.tsx:562-575 (footer switcher); apps/web/app/{hi,ta,te,bn,mr}/page.tsx; apps/web/components/LangLanding.tsx
  - evidence: Probed 8 routes in a live browser for visible language controls. Every one exposes the same footer block — 'Read in:' + Link to / /hi /ta /te /bn /mr — and nothing else. Script census of visible text nodes: /pujas 5 indic of 337 total; /temples 5/443; /darshan 5/319; /store 5/421; /donations 5/221; /content 5/264; /panchang 5/250. In every case the 5 indic strings are the switcher's own labels (हिन्दी, தமிழ், తెలుగు, বাংলা, मराठी). The destinations are standalone pages of 56-109 lines (ta 4,599 bytes, te 4,186, bn 4,002, mr 3,976, hi 6,338), all rendering components/LangLanding.tsx. Direct HTTP census of the landing pages: /hi 28 indic of 147, /ta 28/148, /te 28/148, /bn 28/148, /mr 28/148 — ~19%, the other 118 strings being the shared English header and footer. Live browser check of /hi: 93 outbound links, of which 5 are locale-scoped — and those 5 are the footer switcher repeating itself. Every product link (/pujas, /darshan, /panchang, /gaushala, /gurukuls, /nri, /ask-pandit, /auth/login) exits to English. The Tamil page states this in its own footer text: 'முழுமையான தமிழ் மொழிபெயர்ப்பு முன்னேற்றத்தில் உள்ளது. பெரும்பாலான பக்கங்கள் தற்போது ஆங்கிலத்தில் உள்ளன' — 'Full Tamil translation is in progress. Most pages are currently in English.' LangLanding.tsx's header comment concedes the design: 'Routes that haven't been translated link back to canonical English instead of fake /lang/* subpaths.'
  - customer_impact: A Tamil or Bengali speaker clicks their language, reads one translated screen, clicks any call-to-action, and is in English with no way back — the switcher is not present on the English page they land on except as the same five links, which return them to the same dead end. The footer advertises six languages on every page of the product, which reads as a promise of a localized app. Users who cannot read English will convert at near zero after this loop, and the ones who notice the disclaimer learn the product overstated itself.
  - fix: Two honest options. (a) Stop advertising: replace the footer 'Read in:' block with a single link to a language page that states plainly which surfaces are translated, so the claim matches delivery. (b) Make it real: introduce a [locale] route segment, move the landing content into it, and have LangLanding's feature hrefs emit locale-prefixed paths so the session stays in-language. Do not ship (b) partially — a locale prefix that silently drops to English is worse than today. Given I-01, (a) is the correct immediate move.

### [I-03] P1/i18n/S The one working language switcher translates 3 strings out of 264; 15 of 18 authored Hindi labels are never read
  - where: apps/web/components/LanguageSwitcher.tsx (CONTENT_LABELS export); apps/web/components/ContentPageHeader.tsx:24-60
  - evidence: Drove the /content switcher in a live browser. Before: 264 visible strings, 5 indic. Opened the dropdown (1 switcher button found), selected हिन्दी. After: 264 visible strings, 8 indic. Delta = 3 translated strings; 4 strings changed in total, one of which was a running clock. localStorage poojly_language = 'hi' and the choice survives navigating away and back to /content (indic returns to 8). It has no effect elsewhere: after switching, /pujas measured 5 indic of 337 — unchanged. Cause: CONTENT_LABELS.hi authors 18 keys (pageTitle, pageSubtitle, searchPlaceholder, todaysFeatured, todaysFeaturedSubtitle, curatedCollections, allContent, aarti, chalisa, mantra, bhajan, stories, title, deity, type, views, likes, tracks, noContentFound, tryDifferentSearch, checkBackSoon), and ContentPageHeader.tsx reads exactly three of them — t.pageTitle (line 32), t.pageSubtitle (line 35), t.searchPlaceholder (line 57). The component is the only consumer of CONTENT_LABELS. Separately, LanguageSwitcher's own type is `SupportedLanguage = 'en' | 'hi'` — two languages, while the footer offers six.
  - customer_impact: A user who finds the /content switcher gets a Hindi hero banner over an entirely English page: the category tabs (Aarti, Chalisa, Mantra, Bhajan, Stories), the table headers (Title, Deity, Type, Views, Likes), and the empty states all stay English despite Hindi copy for each already sitting in the file. This is the most damaging kind of partial translation — it looks deliberate, so the user assumes the rest is untranslatable rather than unwired.
  - fix: Thread CONTENT_LABELS[language] from ContentPageHeader down to the /content list body (lift `language` into a context or pass it to the list component) and consume the remaining 15 keys at their render sites. This is a wiring change against copy that already exists — no new translation needed. Widen SupportedLanguage past 'en'|'hi' only after I-01 lands.

### [I-04] P1/i18n/M API error localization: 150 authored strings, 5 languages, 2 call sites, 3 reachable codes — 0 of 40 probed responses localized
  - where: apps/api/src/lib/error-i18n.ts; apps/api/src/routes/bookings.ts:19,677-686,1743-1748; apps/api/src/routes/*.ts (87 files)
  - evidence: error-i18n.ts defines 30 error codes across 5 catalogs (en/hi/ta/te/kn) = 150 authored strings, plus a genuinely correct q-weighted Accept-Language parser (pickLang splits 'hi-IN,hi;q=0.9,en;q=0.8', sorts by q, matches primary subtag) and a buildErrorResponse envelope helper. Grepping the whole API for consumers returns exactly one route file: bookings.ts imports it at line 19 and calls it at lines 681 and 1747. Both call sites are guarded by `if (failure.code)` where failure comes from classifyCreateBookingError (line 578), which can only ever return three codes — TEMPLE_NOT_VERIFIED, SLOT_UNAVAILABLE, PUJA_UNAVAILABLE — and returns a bare status with no code otherwise. So 3 of 30 codes are reachable: 12 of the 120 non-English strings can be delivered, 108 cannot. buildErrorResponse has zero callers anywhere. Against that, `grep "error: '...'" routes/*.ts` counts 683 hardcoded English error literals across 87 route files (top repeats: 'Invalid input' x25, 'Validation Error' x20, 'Invalid request body' x18, 'Invalid body' x18, 'TEMPLE_MANAGER role required' x16, 'Forbidden' x15, 'Unauthorized' x14). Empirically probed 8 error surfaces (bad password, missing token, invalid booking body, bad puja id, bad query, empty store order, empty donation, admin forbidden) x 5 languages, sending BOTH `Accept-Language: xx-IN,xx;q=0.9` AND `?lang=xx`: 40 responses, 40 English, 0 localized. Sample — POST /api/v1/auth/login with wrong password returned 'Unauthorized' identically for en/hi/ta/te/kn.
  - customer_impact: Every error a devotee can actually hit — wrong password, expired OTP, payment failure, empty cart, refund window closed — is English. Errors are precisely where a user who is struggling in a second language most needs their own; a Hindi speaker who mistypes a password gets no recoverable signal. The 108 unreachable strings also mean any future audit of 'do we support Hindi errors?' answers yes from the file and no from the wire.
  - fix: Add a Fastify onSend hook or error-serializer that resolves lang once per request via the existing pickLang(request.headers['accept-language'], request.query.lang) and maps any `{error, code}` payload through localizedError, so localization is structural rather than per-call-site. Then migrate the 683 literals to codes incrementally, highest-traffic routes first (auth.ts, payments.ts, bookings.ts, store-orders.ts) — the hook means each migrated literal is localized the moment it gets a code. Also reconcile the language set: error-i18n covers en/hi/ta/te/kn while the web ships hi/ta/te/bn/mr landings, so bn and mr users can never get a localized error and kn users have no landing page (see I-11).

### [I-05] P1/i18n/S <html lang> is hardcoded to "en" everywhere, which makes the Devanagari font rule a selector that can never match
  - where: apps/web/app/layout.tsx:257-260; apps/web/app/globals.css:189-192,245-252
  - evidence: layout.tsx line 258 is a literal `lang="en"` on the root <html>; nothing in the codebase writes that attribute. Verified in the browser: lang="en" on /, /pujas, /temples, /content and also on /hi, /ta, /te, /bn, /mr (dir was null on all). globals.css:249 defines `html[lang="hi"] { --font-body: var(--font-devanagari); --font-display: var(--font-devanagari); }` — dead code, because lang is never 'hi'. The comment directly above it (line 245) diagnoses the exact failure it is meant to prevent: 'Cabinet Grotesk / Satoshi have no Devanagari glyphs, so the browser would fall back to a system serif and break the page rhythm.' Measured at runtime with document.fonts.ready awaited: `getComputedStyle(document.body).fontFamily` resolves to `Satoshi, Inter, sans-serif` — no Indic-capable family in the chain. Note globals.css:115 defines a `--font-family-sans` that DOES include 'Noto Sans Devanagari', but the variable actually applied to body is `--font-body` (line 190), which does not. LangLanding.tsx:28 sets lang={props.langCode} on <main>, not <html>, so the locale pages do not satisfy the selector either.
  - customer_impact: Two harms. Assistive tech: screen readers announce every Devanagari, Tamil and Bengali string on the page using English pronunciation rules, which renders them unintelligible — this affects the temple names, the switcher labels, and the whole /hi..//mr landing content. Search: hreflang alternates are declared in metadata while every page self-reports as English, which contradicts the alternates and undercuts the locale pages' indexing. Rendering: the Devanagari webfont the app pays to declare is never activated, so Hindi text falls to whatever the client OS provides.
  - fix: Make lang dynamic. Minimum viable: in app/{hi,ta,te,bn,mr}/layout.tsx (or via the root layout reading the first path segment) set `<html lang={locale}>`. Once I-01 lands, drive it from the resolved locale. Independently, fix the font chain so it does not depend on the selector at all — append the Indic families to the base `--font-body`/`--font-display` declarations at globals.css:189-190 rather than swapping them under html[lang="hi"]. Add a test asserting document.documentElement.lang matches the route locale.

### [I-06] P1/i18n/S 11 of 20 temple names are in scripts the app loads no webfont for; glyph coverage is delegated entirely to the user's OS
  - where: apps/web/app/layout.tsx:57-70 (next/font declarations); apps/web/app/globals.css:189-192
  - evidence: The app loads exactly three webfonts via next/font: Noto_Sans (subsets ['latin']), Noto_Sans_Devanagari (subsets ['devanagari']), Crimson_Pro (subsets ['latin']) — plus Satoshi/Cabinet Grotesk from the Fontshare CDN, neither of which has Indic glyphs. Tallied temples.name_local by Unicode block across the 20 seeded rows: Devanagari 9, Tamil 3, Telugu 2, Odia 2, Gujarati 2, Bengali 1, Kannada 1. So 11 of 20 are in a script with no webfont declared at any level. Measured rendering on this Windows 10 box with document.fonts.ready awaited, per-character advance at 32px: devanagari 11.71, tamil 21.55, telugu 19.76, bengali 13.56, kannada 22.11, gujarati 13.91, odia 12.26, latin control 16.07. The spread confirms real glyphs, not tofu — fallback boxes would produce a single identical advance across all seven. That is the OS (Nirmala UI et al.) rescuing the page, not the application.
  - customer_impact: Rendering correctness for 11 of 20 temple names — and for Tamil, Telugu, Bengali, Kannada and Gujarati anywhere else they appear — depends on fonts the app never ships. This machine happens to have them. A budget Android handset, a Linux kiosk, or a stripped container will show replacement boxes for exactly the temple names most likely to matter to a South Indian or Bengali user. It is untestable from a developer's Windows or Mac laptop, so it will surface as user reports rather than in QA. Note this only bites once I-07 is fixed and the names are actually rendered.
  - fix: Add the needed subsets to next/font: Noto_Sans_Tamil, Noto_Sans_Telugu, Noto_Sans_Bengali, Noto_Sans_Kannada, Noto_Sans_Gujarati, Noto_Sans_Oriya, and append all of them (plus the existing Devanagari) to the base --font-body and --font-display chains in globals.css:189-190 so coverage is unconditional rather than gated on a locale selector. Use `display: 'swap'` and rely on unicode-range so a Latin-only page downloads none of them — the cost is near zero for English users. Verify on a container with no system Indic fonts installed.

### [I-07] P1/i18n/S The API ships correct-script localized names for 20/20 temples; the web renders 0 of them
  - where: apps/web/app/temples/[slug]/page.tsx:65; apps/web/app/temples (list); DB temples.name_local, content_items.title_local
  - evidence: The database is fully populated and correct: 20/20 temples have name_local, each in the regionally appropriate script — 'மீனாட்சி அம்மன் கோயில்' (Meenakshi, Tamil), 'తిరుమల తిరుపతి దేవస్థానం' (Tirupati, Telugu), 'দক্ষিণেশ্বর কালীবাড়ি' (Dakshineswar, Bengali), 'ଜଗନ୍ନାଥ ମନ୍ଦିର' (Jagannath, Odia), 'ಇಸ್ಕಾನ್ ದೇವಾಲಯ' (ISKCON Bangalore, Kannada), 'દ્વારકાધીશ મંદિર' (Dwarkadhish, Gujarati), 'काशी विश्वनाथ मंदिर' (Kashi Vishwanath, Devanagari). 20/20 content_items have title_local. The API already serializes it: GET /api/v1/temples?limit=20 returns a `nameLocal` key on every row, populated 20/20 — confirmed on the wire. The web then discards it. Live check of the /temples page body text: 0 of 20 nameLocal values present. Temple detail /temples/tirumala-tirupati: 'తిరుమల తిరుపతి దేవస్థానం' rendered = false. Cause is visible in source — apps/web/app/temples/[slug]/page.tsx:65 hardcodes `nameLocal: null`. Grepping all nameLocal render sites in apps/web finds it used only on /deity/[slug], /pujas/home-puja, /pujas/[slug] and /pujas/[slug]/temples — never for temples. title_local is rendered in exactly one place, app/content/[slug]/page.tsx:420. The ai_translation_cache table exists with 0 rows.
  - customer_impact: This is the single highest-leverage fix in the area and the most frustrating finding: the localized content already exists, is already correct, is already paid for in payload bytes on every temple request, and is thrown away by the client. A Tamil devotee looking for Meenakshi Amman sees only the romanized 'Meenakshi Amman Temple' when the app is already holding 'மீனாட்சி அம்மன் கோயில்'. It also wastes bandwidth on every /temples call for data nothing consumes.
  - fix: Render it. Delete the hardcoded `nameLocal: null` at apps/web/app/temples/[slug]/page.tsx:65 and pass through the API value, then add a secondary line under the temple name on both the list card and the detail hero — the exact pattern already used at app/pujas/[slug]/page.tsx:293-294 (`{puja.nameLocal && <p className="...">{puja.nameLocal}</p>}`). Wrap it in `<span lang={scriptLocaleFor(temple)}>` so screen readers switch voice. Do the same for content_items.title_local on the /content list. Ship I-06 alongside so the glyphs are guaranteed. No translation work and no backend change required.

### [I-08] P2/i18n/M Accept-Language is never negotiated — no i18n config, no middleware locale logic, no redirect
  - where: apps/web/next.config.ts; apps/web/middleware.ts
  - evidence: grep for i18n|locale|lang in next.config.ts returns nothing — there is no `i18n` block and no locale configuration of any kind. Same grep against middleware.ts returns nothing: the middleware does no locale detection, no cookie read, no redirect. Verified empirically with three browser contexts carrying `Accept-Language: hi-IN,hi;q=0.9`, `ta-IN,ta;q=0.9`, and `en-US`: all three landed on /pujas with no redirect (`location` header absent), htmlLang=en, and an identical 5 indic strings out of 337. The stack does have a correct Accept-Language parser — pickLang in apps/api/src/lib/error-i18n.ts — but it lives in the API and is called from two places in one route file (see I-04). Note that /hi, /ta, /te, /bn and /mr exist as routes, so the destinations for a redirect are already there.
  - customer_impact: A user whose phone and browser are configured entirely in Hindi or Tamil — which is the norm for the target market — gets English by default with no signal the product has anything else, because nothing reads the one header that states their preference. Combined with the switcher living in the page footer (I-02), the localized landing pages are effectively undiscoverable to the users they were built for.
  - fix: Add locale detection to middleware.ts: parse Accept-Language, honour an explicit override cookie first (so a user who chooses English stays in English), and for a supported match on a bare '/' request either redirect to /{locale} or set a locale cookie the app reads. Reuse the pickLang logic from apps/api/src/lib/error-i18n.ts — move it to packages/shared so web and API share one implementation and one supported-language list. Never redirect on non-root paths until locale-scoped product routes exist (I-02), or users will be bounced to a landing page mid-flow.

### [I-09] P2/i18n/M Multilingual help center ships 6 articles against its own stated target of 300, is unlinked from /help, and 5 of 6 are unreviewed machine translations shown without disclosure
  - where: apps/web/lib/help-articles.ts; apps/web/app/help/articles/[lang]/[slug]/page.tsx; apps/web/app/help/page.tsx
  - evidence: help-articles.ts header comment states 'Target: 50 articles x 6 languages (en/hi/ta/te/bn/mr) = 300 total.' Actual content parsed from the file: 1 distinct slug, 6 articles, exactly one per language — 2% of the stated target. The schema field `reviewed_by?: string | null` carries the file's own comment 'null = pending review (non-en seed translations)', and 5 of the 6 articles have `reviewed_by: null` — i.e. every non-English article is an unreviewed seed translation. The rendering page (app/help/articles/[lang]/[slug]/page.tsx) never reads reviewed_by, so nothing marks these as unverified; they render with the same authority as reviewed English copy, with `content-language` metadata and hreflang alternates asserting them as proper localizations. All six URLs return 200 (verified /help/articles/{en,hi,ta,te,bn,mr}/how-to-book-a-puja). The /help index page is 255 lines and contains zero references to help/articles, HELP_LANGS or help-articles — so the localized articles are reachable only by direct URL or sitemap. Separately, apps/web/lib/support.ts:694 tells users 'The app is available in English, Hindi, Tamil, Telugu, Kannada, Marathi, and Bengali... Switch from the language selector in the footer' — that names Kannada, which has no landing page at all.
  - customer_impact: Help content is where a confused user in their second language lands, and unreviewed machine translation of instructions about payments and refunds can actively mislead — a mistranslated refund window or cancellation step causes a support ticket or a chargeback, not just confusion. Because nothing discloses review status, neither users nor the support team can tell which articles are trustworthy. And since /help never links to them, the 2% that does exist is invisible anyway.
  - fix: Three separate changes. (1) Surface review status: render a visible 'Machine translated — pending review' badge whenever reviewed_by is null, and set `robots: noindex` on those articles so unreviewed copy does not become the indexed answer. (2) Link them: add a language row to app/help/page.tsx driven by HELP_LANGS so the articles are reachable. (3) Correct the claim in lib/support.ts:694 — it lists seven languages including Kannada, which has no landing page; make the string match what exists. Either revise the 300-article target in the file header to a real commitment or remove it, so the gap is not mistaken for planned work in flight.

### [I-10] P3/i18n/S INR currency formatting is correct; 23 bare toLocaleString() calls silently take the server's en-US default
  - where: apps/web/lib/pricing.ts:200-216 (correct); 23 sites incl. app/vedas/[slug]/page.tsx:79, app/puranas/page.tsx:98, app/sacred-rivers/page.tsx:71, app/sacred-mountains/page.tsx:82, app/astrology/view/page.tsx:291
  - evidence: The money path is right and worth saying so. formatPrice uses `new Intl.NumberFormat('en-IN', {style:'currency', currency:'INR'})`; verified output 110000 -> 'Rs 1,10,000' and 225000 -> 'Rs 2,25,000' — correct Indian lakh grouping, not 110,000. Across apps/web there are 230 en-IN references against 7 en-US, and all 7 en-US uses are legitimate (USD/GBP branches in provider/BookingRow.tsx and EarningsCard.tsx, a weekday-only DateTimeFormat, and canonical URL keys). Live scan of rendered pages found money strings uniformly correct: /pujas Rs 1,100 / Rs 11,000 / Rs 2,500; /store Rs 1,299 / Rs 1,899; /astrology Rs 50 / Rs 80. The defect is narrow: 23 call sites use bare `toLocaleString()` with no locale argument, which resolves to the runtime default — measured on this stack as en-US (`Intl.DateTimeFormat().resolvedOptions().locale` = 'en-US'), so 1234567 formats as '1,234,567' instead of '12,34,567'. All 23 are non-money counts: verse counts, mantra totals, river lengths, mountain heights, review and consultation counts, admin timestamps. No financial impact. Two are in client components (app/astrology/view/page.tsx, AstrologyClient.tsx), where the server renders with en-US and the browser re-renders with the user's locale — a latent hydration-mismatch source for any non-en-US visitor.
  - customer_impact: An Indian reader sees '1,234,567 mantras' where they expect '12,34,567' — jarring on content pages whose whole purpose is cultural authenticity, though it never touches a price or a payable amount. The two client-component sites can additionally throw a React hydration warning for visitors whose browser locale groups differently from the server's.
  - fix: Pass the locale explicitly at all 23 sites: `n.toLocaleString('en-IN')`. Better, export a `formatCount(n)` helper next to formatPrice in lib/pricing.ts and use it, so the locale lives in one place. Add an ESLint rule (no-restricted-syntax on CallExpression[callee.property.name='toLocaleString'][arguments.length=0]) to prevent regressions — this is exactly the class of bug that reappears.

### [I-11] P2/i18n/S Four different, mutually inconsistent lists of supported languages; preferredLanguage is validated and stored but read by nothing
  - where: apps/api/src/routes/users.ts:14; apps/api/src/lib/error-i18n.ts:15; apps/web/app/layout.tsx:562-575; apps/web/components/LanguageSwitcher.tsx:7; apps/web/lib/support.ts:694
  - evidence: Four incompatible definitions of 'supported': users.ts:14 validates `z.enum(['en','hi','ta','te','bn','mr','gu','kn','ml','pa'])` — 10 languages. error-i18n.ts:15 declares `SupportedLang = 'en'|'hi'|'ta'|'te'|'kn'` — 5, including kn but excluding bn and mr. The footer switcher offers en/hi/ta/te/bn/mr — 6, excluding kn. LanguageSwitcher.tsx:7 declares `SupportedLanguage = 'en'|'hi'` — 2. And support.ts:694 tells users there are 7 (adds Kannada to the footer's six). Routes exist for /hi /ta /te /bn /mr; /gu, /kn, /ml and /pa do not exist, so 4 of the 10 values users.ts happily accepts have no destination. Consequence: bn and mr users can select a language with a landing page but can never receive a localized API error; kn users can receive a localized error but have no landing page to reach. On the storage side, preferred_language is accepted at users.ts:14, persisted, and returned by auth.ts:425/673/780 and users.ts:89/145 — and grepping every consumer in apps/web and apps/api shows nothing reads it to alter output. The only non-test consumer is jobs/weekly-digest.ts:298, which selects the column. 22 of 23 seeded users have preferred_language='hi'. The profile page (app/profile/page.tsx:350) presents it as a settings control, and the API returns 200 on save.
  - customer_impact: A user sets Preferred Language to Tamil in their profile, the app confirms the save, and literally nothing changes anywhere, ever — a settings control that reports success while doing nothing is a direct trust cost, and it is the kind of thing users test early. The four-way list mismatch also guarantees that any future partial fix lands inconsistently: adding Bengali error strings would not help Bengali users unless someone notices error-i18n's list differs from the footer's.
  - fix: Define the supported-language list exactly once in packages/shared (alongside the shared pickLang from I-08) and import it at all five sites, so the enum, the error catalogs, the switcher and the footer cannot drift. Narrow users.ts:14 to languages that actually have a destination. Then make preferredLanguage load-bearing: on login, seed the locale cookie from user.preferredLanguage so the stored preference drives rendering once I-01 lands. Until it does, either remove the profile control or label it 'coming soon' — the page already uses that pattern for avatar upload two fields above.

## AREA: i18n   score=16

### [I18N-01] P0/i18n/M Footer advertises 5 Indian languages on every page; all 5 lead to a ~15%-translated dead end whose every onward link is English or 404
  - where: apps/web/app/layout.tsx:567-571 (footer links), apps/web/app/{hi,ta,te,bn,mr}/page.tsx, apps/web/components/OnboardingModal.tsx:38-42
  - evidence: Playwright DOM scan of every visible leaf text node. /hi: 146 visible strings, 24 Devanagari, 114 latin-only = 16.4% translated (repeat run: 156/24 = 15.4%). /ta: 157 strings, 23 Tamil = 14.6%. /te: 157/23 = 14.6%. /bn: 157/23 = 14.6%. Untranslated on all five: ["Skip to main content","Darsh","ya","Worship","Library","Astrology","Yatra","Shop"] — i.e. the entire global nav/footer chrome. Click-through coverage measured: /pujas 2 Devanagari of 290 strings (0.7%), homepage 4/411, /dashboard 2/177, /profile 2/163. Localized subroute probe, 7/7 404: /hi/pujas 404, /hi/darshan 404, /hi/panchang 404, /ta/pujas 404, /te/pujas 404, /bn/pujas 404, /mr/pujas 404. OnboardingModal offers ta/te/bn/mr with active:false yet the footer links them unconditionally.
  - customer_impact: A Hindi- or Tamil-speaking devotee — the core stated audience, and 22 of 23 seeded users prefer 'hi' — clicks 'हिन्दी' in the footer, lands on a page that is 85% English chrome, taps 'पूजा बुक करें', and is dropped into a fully English booking flow. The language promise is broken at the exact moment money is involved. For a devotional product in India, language IS the buying decision, so this reads as a false capability claim on every page view rather than an incomplete feature.
  - fix: Two options, pick by honesty budget. (a) Ship it: add apps/web/lib/i18n/hi.json (374 keys) and make t.ts locale-aware (see I18N-02) — because nav+footer are already key-ified this alone takes /hi from 16% to roughly full chrome coverage plus the 8 already-wired pages. (b) Stop claiming it: remove the ta/te/bn/mr footer links until their locale files exist, and relabel the /hi link so it does not imply a translated product. Do (b) this week regardless; (a) is the real fix.

### [I18N-02] P1/i18n/M The translation layer is welded to English — lib/t.ts hard-imports en.json and no call site can request another locale
  - where: apps/web/lib/t.ts:1-30; apps/web/lib/i18n/ (contains only en.json)
  - evidence: t.ts is 30 lines. Line 1 is `import en from './i18n/en.json'`; build() closes over that module constant (`const dict = en[ns]`). getTranslations/useTranslations/t all take a namespace and NO locale argument, so no caller can ever select a language. `ls apps/web/lib/i18n/` returns exactly one file: en.json (374 keys across 14 namespaces: nav 32, common 34, home 80, temples 19, pujas 21, darshan 12, donations 20, content 18, store 17, auth 33, upgrade 27, yatra 13, terms 17, footer 31). Adoption: 8 of 368 page.tsx files call the layer (home, pujas, store, darshan, donations, upgrade, yatra/destinations, auth/login) plus layout.tsx which routes the whole nav and footer through it (`tNav`/`tFooter` at layout.tsx:246-247). No middleware locale routing (apps/web/middleware.ts has zero locale/lang/Accept-Language references) and no i18n block in next.config.ts.
  - customer_impact: The expensive part of internationalisation — extracting 374 strings from JSX and key-ifying the global chrome — is already paid for, but delivers zero customer value because there is no second dictionary and no way to select one. Every future translation effort will stall on this same 30-line file.
  - fix: Give t.ts a locale parameter: `const DICTS = { en, hi, ta, te, bn, mr }` with per-key fallback to en, and resolve the active locale from a source of truth (a `app/[locale]/` route segment is cleanest; a cookie set by the switcher is the cheap version). Then author hi.json first — 374 strings is roughly one translator-day and it lights up the entire nav/footer plus all 8 wired pages simultaneously. Add a CI check that every non-en locale file has the same key set as en.json so drift is caught at build time.

### [I18N-03] P1/i18n/S 756 of 758 API error responses are English-only, despite a complete, working 5-language error catalog sitting unused
  - where: apps/api/src/lib/error-i18n.ts (catalog); apps/api/src/server.ts:436 (the global error handler that ignores it); apps/api/src/routes/bookings.ts:677,1743 (the only two consumers)
  - evidence: 758 `reply.code(4xx/5xx).send` occurrences across 81 route files; exactly 1 file imports error-i18n (bookings.ts), used at 2 call sites, reachable for only 3 codes (TEMPLE_NOT_VERIFIED, SLOT_UNAVAILABLE, PUJA_UNAVAILABLE per classifyCreateBookingError at bookings.ts:578-591). The catalog itself is complete and PROVEN WORKING — live probe of POST /api/v1/bookings/book with a bogus slot returned, per Accept-Language: hi -> {"error":"चुना गया समय स्लॉट अब उपलब्ध नहीं है।"}; ta -> "தேர்ந்தெடுக்கப்பட்ட நேர இடம் இனி கிடைக்கவில்லை."; te -> "ఎంచుకున్న సమయ స్లాట్ ఇకపై అందుబాటులో లేదు."; kn -> "ಆಯ್ಕೆ ಮಾಡಿದ ಸಮಯ ಸ್ಲಾಟ್ ಇನ್ನು ಲಭ್ಯವಿಲ್ಲ." Everything else is English regardless of Accept-Language or ?lang=: login wrong-password with 'Accept-Language: hi-IN' -> {"message":"Invalid email or password"}; with '?lang=hi' -> identical; validation error with 'Accept-Language: hi' -> {"message":"Invalid email address"}; GET /temples/nope with 'Accept-Language: ta' -> {"error":"Temple not found"}. Root cause is structural: the single global handler at server.ts:436 is declared `(err, _req, reply)` — it discards the request, so it cannot read Accept-Language even in principle.
  - customer_impact: A Hindi user who fat-fingers a password, hits a validation error, or loses a slot gets an English wall. Error text is exactly where a non-English user is most stressed and least able to self-recover, so this converts recoverable friction into abandoned bookings and support tickets. The 30-code catalog was written and paid for and reaches 0.26% of error responses.
  - fix: Wire the catalog once at the choke point instead of 758 times. Change server.ts:436 to accept `req`, compute `const lang = pickLang(req.headers['accept-language'], (req.query as any)?.lang)`, and emit `buildErrorResponse(code, lang)`. Map the common Fastify/Zod failure shapes to catalog codes so validation and 401/404 inherit localisation for free. Keep the two bookings.ts call sites as-is — they already do the right thing and prove the pattern.

### [I18N-04] P1/i18n/S Five layers disagree about which languages exist; bn and mr are offered in the UI but silently fall back to English, while kn is supported but offered nowhere
  - where: apps/api/prisma/schema.prisma (preferred_language enum), apps/web/app/layout.tsx:567-571, apps/web/components/LanguageSwitcher.tsx:8, apps/api/src/lib/error-i18n.ts:14-16,217, apps/web/lib/i18n/
  - evidence: Five different language sets in one product. DB enum (queried from pg_enum): 10 values — en, hi, ta, te, bn, mr, gu, kn, ml, pa. Web footer: 6 (en, hi, ta, te, bn, mr). LanguageSwitcher.tsx:8 `export type SupportedLanguage = 'en' | 'hi'`: 2. error-i18n.ts:16 SUPPORTED_LANGS: 5 — en, hi, ta, te, kn (includes kn, EXCLUDES bn and mr). Actual locale files: 1. The mismatch is not theoretical — same live booking probe: Accept-Language bn -> {"error":"The selected time slot is no longer available."} (English), mr -> English, while kn returned correct Kannada. So the two languages the footer actively links are the two the API cannot localize, and the one the API can localize is exposed nowhere.
  - customer_impact: A Bengali or Marathi user is offered their language in the footer, and every API error they hit silently answers in English with no indication anything degraded. localizedError()'s fallback is deliberately silent, so this failure is invisible in logs and will never be noticed until a user complains. Meanwhile Kannada translation work is stranded and unreachable.
  - fix: Pick one canonical list and derive the rest from it. Export a single SUPPORTED_LOCALES const from packages/shared, and have the Prisma enum, the footer, LanguageSwitcher, and error-i18n's CATALOGS all reference it. Add bn and mr to CATALOGS (30 strings each) or drop them from the footer — do not ship a link to a language the backend cannot speak. Add a unit test asserting Object.keys(CATALOGS) equals the UI-offered set, so the next language added cannot drift.

### [I18N-05] P2/i18n/S The only in-app language control translates 3 strings on 1 page; 15 of its 18 Hindi labels are dead code
  - where: apps/web/components/LanguageSwitcher.tsx:150-197 (CONTENT_LABELS), apps/web/components/ContentPageHeader.tsx:24,32,35,56
  - evidence: LanguageSwitcher is imported by exactly one file in the whole app (ContentPageHeader.tsx:5), so it renders only on /content. Measured on /content: with poojly_language unset, 283 visible strings / 2 Devanagari; after setting poojly_language='hi' and reloading, 283 strings / 5 Devanagari. DELTA = 3 strings (1.1% of the page). Cause: CONTENT_LABELS defines 18 Hindi keys but ContentPageHeader consumes exactly three of them — t.pageTitle (line 32), t.pageSubtitle (line 35), t.searchPlaceholder (line 56). The other 15 translated labels (aarti, chalisa, mantra, bhajan, stories, title, deity, type, views, likes, tracks, noContentFound, tryDifferentSearch, checkBackSoon, curatedCollections) are never rendered anywhere. With the preference persisted, other pages are unaffected: homepage 4 Devanagari of 411 strings, /pujas 2 of 291, and 0 language switchers present on either.
  - customer_impact: A user who finds the globe icon on /content, switches to हिन्दी, and navigates anywhere else sees the setting evaporate with no explanation — classic 'the toggle is decorative' distrust. The preference is written to localStorage under 'poojly_language' and read by nothing outside that one header.
  - fix: Once t.ts is locale-aware (I18N-02), delete CONTENT_LABELS entirely and move its three live strings into the `content` namespace of en.json/hi.json, which already has 18 keys reserved. Render the switcher from the global layout (header or footer) rather than one page header, and have it write the locale to the same source of truth the server reads, so a switch persists across navigation.

### [I18N-06] P2/i18n/S No webfont covers any Indic script — Devanagari, Tamil, Telugu and Bengali all render from OS fallback, and the Noto Sans Devanagari that IS loaded is never referenced by the headings
  - where: apps/web/app/layout.tsx:57-75,259 (next/font declarations), apps/web/app/globals.css:115-118,192
  - evidence: In-browser measurement on each locale landing page. On /hi, /ta, /te and /bn alike: `document.fonts` filtered to status==='loaded' returned [] (zero activated webfonts), and the h1 computed font-family resolved to `Satoshi, Inter, sans-serif` — none of which ship Devanagari, Tamil, Telugu or Bengali glyphs. Canvas width comparison confirms no webfont coverage: rendering 'देवनागरी' at 32px in the page font measured 110px vs 110px in a deliberately-missing font; 'தமிழ்' 95px vs 95px; 'తెలుగు' 102px vs 102px; 'বাংলা' 74px vs 74px — identical in every case, meaning the same OS fallback served both. Source confirms the gap: layout.tsx loads only Noto_Sans(subsets:['latin']), Noto_Sans_Devanagari(subsets:['devanagari']) and Crimson_Pro(subsets:['latin']) — no Tamil, Telugu or Bengali face at all. And the Devanagari face that IS declared is exposed as --font-noto-devanagari, which globals.css only wires into --font-devanagari (line 192) and one .reader rule (line 671); the heading stack never references it, so it is downloaded-and-unused. globals.css:115 lists 'Noto Sans Devanagari' as a bare family name, which only resolves if the user happens to have it installed locally.
  - customer_impact: Indic text renders in whatever the device happens to have. On Windows/Chrome that is Nirmala UI and it looks acceptable — which is exactly why this has gone unnoticed — but on a stripped Linux, an older/low-end Android, or a kiosk without Indic fonts the same page renders as tofu boxes. Typography is also inconsistent with the brand on every Indic page since it is whatever the OS picked, not Satoshi. Low-end Android is a large share of the Indian devotional market.
  - fix: Add Noto_Sans_Tamil, Noto_Sans_Telugu and Noto_Sans_Bengali via next/font alongside the existing Devanagari face, and reference the resulting CSS variables from the font stacks that headings actually use — put the script faces into --font-family-sans/--font-family-display, not only into the unused --font-devanagari. Verify per locale by asserting document.fonts contains a loaded family and that the canvas width differs from a known-missing font. Until the faces ship, the ta/te/bn links should stay hidden per I18N-01(b).

### [I18N-07] P2/data-integrity/S preferredLanguage is a write-only field — set at signup, editable in the profile UI, returned by the API, and read by nothing that renders; the demo path also reports it wrongly
  - where: apps/api/src/routes/auth.ts:625 (and the parallel demo literals at 390, 731, 1117), apps/api/src/routes/users.ts:14,89,145, apps/web/app/profile/page.tsx:344-360, apps/api/src/jobs/weekly-digest.ts:298
  - evidence: DB query: 22 of 23 users have preferred_language='hi'; the enum admits 10 values. The /profile page renders a 'Preferred Language' <select> that PATCHes users.preferredLanguage, and users.ts:14 validates it against all 10 codes — but grep across apps/web finds zero reads that select a translation from it, and across apps/api the only non-echo consumer is weekly-digest.ts, which selects the column at line 298 and then never references it when composing the email. Separately, a live inconsistency: POST /api/v1/auth/login for devotee@demo.com returned preferredLanguage:"en" while the DB row for that same email is 'hi' — the synthetic-user branch (auth.ts:625, mirrored at 390/731/1117) hardcodes a literal instead of reading the row. Confirmed for all 8 demo accounts: every one is 'hi' in Postgres.
  - customer_impact: The product asks a user to state their language preference, stores it, and then ignores it everywhere — a promise made in the UI and silently broken. When I18N-02 lands, this field is the natural default locale source, and it will hand back the wrong value for exactly the accounts used in demos and E2E tests, producing an English experience for a user who explicitly chose Hindi.
  - fix: Make preferredLanguage the server-side default locale: read it in the root layout to seed <html lang> and the dictionary, falling back to Accept-Language then 'en'. Fix the synthetic-user branches to echo the stored value (or, better, resolve demo users through the same Prisma read) so the field is never silently rewritten. Add a test asserting login's preferredLanguage matches the users row for a seeded account.

### [I18N-08] P2/seo/S hreflang cluster is wrong on every page: all 6 alternates are emitted from the root layout, so /pujas tells Google its Hindi equivalent is the Hindi homepage
  - where: apps/web/app/layout.tsx:293-299
  - evidence: The alternates are hardcoded in the ROOT layout, so every route emits the identical set. Fetched http://127.0.0.1:3000/pujas and extracted the link tags verbatim: hrefLang="en" -> https://darshya.online, hrefLang="hi" -> https://darshya.online/hi, ta -> /ta, te -> /te, bn -> /bn, mr -> /mr, x-default -> https://darshya.online, then a second overlapping set en-IN -> https://darshya.online, en-US -> https://darshya.online, and a DUPLICATE x-default -> https://darshya.online. So /pujas declares that its Hindi counterpart is the Hindi homepage — while /hi/pujas itself returns 404 (see I18N-01). The sitemap is narrower and more honest: apps/web/app/sitemap.ts:775-798 declares only the 5 locale landings plus /ta/chennai.
  - customer_impact: hreflang requires page-level equivalence and reciprocal return links. Declaring a homepage as the alternate of a category page is an invalid cluster, so Google discards the annotations rather than honouring them — the Hindi landing page loses the ranking benefit it was built for. The duplicated x-default is an outright validation error. Net effect: the one piece of genuine translation work in the product gets no organic discovery.
  - fix: Remove the hardcoded alternates from layout.tsx and emit them per route from each page's generateMetadata.alternates.languages, listing only languages that actually have that page. Today that means the 5 landing pages cross-link each other (as /hi/page.tsx:12-22 already does correctly) and every other route emits no language alternates at all. Emit exactly one x-default, and drop the redundant en-IN/en-US entries that duplicate en.

### [I18N-09] P2/i18n/M The multilingual help centre is one article, not the 300 its own spec claims — and it is unreachable from the UI
  - where: apps/web/lib/help-articles.ts:5 (stated target), :54 (HELP_ARTICLES), apps/web/app/help/page.tsx, apps/web/app/sitemap.ts
  - evidence: The file header states 'Target: 50 articles x 6 languages (en/hi/ta/te/bn/mr) = 300 total.' Parsed contents: 6 article objects, one per language, sharing a single slug 'how-to-book-a-puja' — 2% of the stated target. Live probes confirm the one slug exists in each language (/help/articles/hi/how-to-book-a-puja 200, /ta/... 200, /mr/... 200). Discoverability: grep for 'help/articles' across apps/web matches only inside the article page itself (the 'Read in another language' strip at line 254) — apps/web/app/help/page.tsx contains no reference to HELP_ARTICLES or to /help/articles at all, and sitemap.ts emits no /help/articles entries. The route is reachable only by typing the URL or by already being on one of the six pages.
  - customer_impact: Non-English self-serve support effectively does not exist: a Hindi user with a refund or cancellation question has one article about booking, and no way to find even that from /help. Every other support question routes to an English page or a human — which is the expensive path this feature existed to avoid.
  - fix: Either fill the catalogue or stop advertising it. Near-term: link the language-filtered article list from /help/page.tsx and add /help/articles/[lang]/[slug] to sitemap.ts so the six existing pages are at least discoverable and indexable. Then prioritise the highest-ticket-volume slugs (refunds, cancellation, order tracking) in hi first rather than spreading thin across six languages. Correct the header comment so it states current coverage, not an aspiration a reader will mistake for status.

### [I18N-10] P3/i18n/S No Accept-Language negotiation anywhere in the web tier; a Hindi browser gets the English page with no Content-Language header
  - where: apps/web/middleware.ts, apps/web/next.config.ts, apps/web/app/layout.tsx:258
  - evidence: Request to http://127.0.0.1:3000/ with 'Accept-Language: hi-IN,hi;q=0.9,en;q=0.8' returned 200, Location header absent, <html lang>=en, Content-Language absent. Same for 'ta-IN,ta;q=0.9' (200, no Location). Source confirms there is no mechanism at all: grep for locale/lang/i18n/Accept-Language across apps/web/middleware.ts returns no matches, and next.config.ts has no i18n block. Separately, layout.tsx:258 hardcodes lang="en" on the <html> element, so all five locale routes serve <html lang="en"> (measured on /hi, /ta, /te, /bn and on /dashboard and /profile). Partial mitigation already present: LangLanding.tsx:28 and hi/page.tsx:44 correctly set lang={langCode} on the <main> element, so assistive tech does switch voice inside the translated region.
  - customer_impact: A Hindi-locale browser arriving at darshya.online is never offered the Hindi page that exists — discovery of the localized landing pages depends entirely on scrolling to the footer. The document-level lang="en" also suppresses Chrome's 'translate this page' prompt (the browser believes the page is already in the user's... no — it believes the page is English, and for a Hindi user this is the one signal that would otherwise rescue the experience) and misinforms Google's language detection about the /hi cluster.
  - fix: Set <html lang> from the active locale rather than a literal — trivial once I18N-02 introduces a locale source. Add a middleware rule that, for a first-time visitor to / whose Accept-Language primary tag has a locale file, offers (do not force-redirect, it harms SEO and surprises bilingual users) the localized landing via a dismissible banner, and emit a Content-Language response header matching the served locale.

### [I18N-11] P3/i18n/S Currency and date formatting are correct and consistently Indian — the one genuinely healthy part of this area
  - where: apps/web/lib/pricing.ts:200-215 (formatPrice), plus 53 files using toLocaleDateString('en-IN')
  - evidence: Reported as healthy, with the one real gap noted. formatPrice uses Intl.NumberFormat with locale 'en-IN' for INR (pricing.ts:206-214), which produces correct Indian lakh/crore grouping. Runtime scan of /pujas, /pricing and /nri for any rupee amount of 5+ digits found 1 correctly-grouped value (₹11,000 on /pujas) and 0 defects — no western grouping, no ungrouped amounts. Dates: 60 of 61 toLocaleDateString call sites across 53 files pass 'en-IN' explicitly and ZERO pass 'en-US'. Remaining gap, minor: 11 of 14 toLocaleTimeString calls pass no locale, and 24 bare toLocaleDateString()/toLocaleString() calls across 14 files (astrology/view, vedas, sacred-rivers, sacred-mountains, puranas, mahabharata, org/members, admin/agents, admin/roadmap) inherit the ambient locale — which is the server's during SSR and the visitor's after hydration.
  - customer_impact: Prices and dates read correctly to an Indian user today, which matters more than it sounds — western digit grouping on a ₹1,10,000 donation is an immediate credibility hit and this product avoids it. The residual risk is confined to the 24 locale-less calls, which can render one string on the server and a different one in the browser, producing a React hydration mismatch and a visible flicker on those content pages.
  - fix: No action needed on the currency path. For the stragglers, pass 'en-IN' explicitly to the 11 toLocaleTimeString and 24 bare toLocaleDateString/toLocaleString call sites, or route them through a shared formatDate/formatTime helper in lib/. Add an ESLint rule banning locale-less toLocale* calls so the pattern cannot come back. When I18N-02 lands, that helper is also the seam where date formatting can follow the active locale.

## AREA: notifications   score=15

### [NOTIF-01] P0/resilience/S No scheduler invokes runDailyOps — every time-based notification is dead code in production
  - where: apps/api/src/jobs/master-cron.ts:33-35,328-402; apps/api/src/routes/admin-leads.ts:539,547; .github/workflows/daily-cron.yml
  - evidence: runDailyOps() owns 18 ticks including tickPanchangBlast (daily 5am push), tickAartiNotify (15-min aarti reminder), tickWeeklyDigest, tickDispatchSweep (the only thing that rebroadcasts an expired pandit offer or tells the devotee 'we're re-matching'), tickAutoRefunds and tickWeeklySettlement. It is reachable only from POST /api/v1/admin/jobs/daily-ops (admin JWT) and GET /api/v1/admin/cron/tick (x-cron-token). Repo-wide grep over *.yml/*.mjs/*.md for 'daily-ops', 'cron/tick', 'x-cron-token', 'CRON_TICK_TOKEN' returns ZERO callers — the only hits are the route definitions themselves plus one AUDIT.md mention. The single scheduled workflow, .github/workflows/daily-cron.yml (cron '0 0 * * *'), curls a DIFFERENT endpoint: $API_BASE_URL/api/v1/cron/daily. master-cron.ts:35 documents the real dependency as an out-of-repo service: 'Hit it from cron-job.org (free, every 30 min) or Upstash QStash.'
  - customer_impact: Unless someone has hand-configured cron-job.org outside the repo, no devotee has ever received a panchang push, an aarti reminder, or a weekly digest; no pandit offer that timed out has ever been rebroadcast; no auto-refund has been processed; no weekly payout has been settled. A devotee whose pandit never accepts sits on a paid booking with no notification and no re-match, forever.
  - fix: Add a scheduled GitHub Actions workflow that curls GET /api/v1/admin/cron/tick with the x-cron-token header on a */10 schedule (2000 free minutes/mo easily covers it), and make the existing daily-cron.yml call it too. Then add a boot-time assertion or an /admin/ops row that surfaces 'last daily-ops tick' age so a dead scheduler is visible instead of silent.

### [NOTIF-02] P0/data-integrity/S Digest unsubscribe throws Postgres 42804 on every call, records nothing, and returns 200 "You're unsubscribed"
  - where: apps/api/src/routes/digest.ts:33-45; apps/web/app/account/digest/unsubscribe/page.tsx:26-47
  - evidence: Reproduced the exact statement through the API's own Prisma client against the live DB: `ERROR: column "user_id" is of type uuid but expression is of type text  HINT: You will need to rewrite or cast the expression.` (Code 42804). The same SQL run through a raw pg client succeeds (RETURNING one row), so the table and constraints are fine — it is Prisma's untyped $1 bind. Adding `$1::uuid` makes it succeed (rowsAffected = 1). End-to-end against the running API with a real seeded uuid: `POST /api/v1/digest/unsubscribe {userId:'2b5bd615-1633-4c95-9b09-03efb2b9e429'}` -> `200 {"ok":true,"unsubscribed":true,"note":"recorded best-effort"}`, and `SELECT * FROM "WeeklyDigestOptOut"` immediately after returns `[]`. The catch block at digest.ts:41 swallows the error and the 'note' field is the only trace. The web page treats any 2xx as success and renders 'You're unsubscribed'.
  - customer_impact: Every unsubscribe click is a lie. The user is told they are off the list, the opt-out row is never written, weekly-digest.ts fetchOptOutSet returns an empty set, and they keep receiving the digest. That is a CAN-SPAM / DPDP consent-withdrawal failure, and the user's only recourse is to mark the mail as spam — which poisons the sending domain's reputation for every other transactional email.
  - fix: Change `VALUES ($1, NOW(), $2)` to `VALUES ($1::uuid, NOW(), $2)` at digest.ts:35 (proven fix). Then stop returning 200 from the catch — return 500 so the page shows the error state, and add a regression test that asserts the row exists after the call rather than asserting the status code.

### [NOTIF-03] P0/resilience/S All four outbound channels are unconfigured and nothing in the product can tell you
  - where: apps/api/src/lib/{email,whatsapp,web-push,fcm}.ts; apps/api/src/routes/notifications.ts:85-112
  - evidence: Measured against the running API. `GET /api/v1/notifications/vapid-public-key` -> `{"publicKey":"","enabled":false}`. `GET /api/v1/gift-puja/config` -> `{"paymentEnabled":false,"whatsappEnabled":false,"autoFlowReady":false}`. `POST /api/v1/auth/magic-link/request` -> `{"info":"Email skipped (RESEND_API_KEY not set). Use devLink to sign in."}`. `POST /api/v1/notifications/test-fcm` -> `{"ok":true,"skipped":true,"reason":"FCM not configured"}`. With a real subscription row persisted for the user, `POST /api/v1/notifications/test` -> `{"ok":true,"sent":0,"expired":0,"failed":0}` — sendPush's `{ok:false,skipped:true}` is counted as neither sent, expired, nor failed, so the diagnostic endpoint built for exactly this question answers 'ok'.
